Generalized gradient structures for measure-valued population dynamics and their large-population limit
Jasper Hoeksema and Oliver Tse

Abstract
. We consider the forward Kolmogorov equation corresponding to measure-valued processes stemming from a class of interacting particle systems in population dynamics, including variations of the Bolker-Pacala-Dieckmann-Law model. Under the assumption of detailed balance, we provide a rigorous generalized gradient structure, incorporating the fluxes arising from the birth and death of the particles. Moreover, in the large population limit, we show convergence of the forward Kolmogorov equation to a Liouville equation, which is a transport equation associated with the mean-field limit of the underlying process. In addition, we show convergence of the corresponding gradient structures in the sense of Energy-Dissipation Principles, from which we establish a propagation of chaos result for the particle system and derive a generalized gradient-flow formulation for the mean-field limit.



1.
Introduction

An important goal in theoretical biology and population dynamics is to derive macroscopic equations from microscopic models
[
CFM06
,
FKK09
]
. For many stochastic interacting particle systems involving birth, mutation, and death, these connections have been made rigorous. One such class of particle systems consists of spatially-structured models such as the Bolker-Pacala and Dieckmann-Law (BPDL) model
[
BP97
,
LD00
]
. The dynamics of these particle systems can be described by jump processes on the space of finite positive measures and can be used to derive macroscopic models.

The convergence of such measure-valued jump processes under a mean-field scaling to a large-population limit is shown for example in
[
FM04
]
via martingale techniques, and in
[
FKK09
]
, where an analytic approach to the convergence of rescaled moment equations is used. In both approaches, the limiting evolution is governed by a non-local evolution equation given by

(1.1)

\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}m(y,x)\,u_{t}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y-u_{t}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}c(x,y)\,u_{t}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

We will refer to (
1.1
) as the
mean-field equation
. Here,
u_{t}
represents the limiting density of particles at time
t
, and the parameter functions
m
and
c
are continuous and bounded functions stemming from birth, dispersal, and competition in the BPDL model.

In recent years, there has been considerable activity in studying the mean-field equation (
1.1
) and the BPDL model in more general spaces, allowing for dynamics involving multiple species and combinations of discrete and continuous traits. See for example
[
FKK21
]
for an overview of existing models, where instead of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
the underlying space is an arbitrary locally compact Polish space. However, convergence in the large-population limit is not considered.

Meanwhile, powerful variational tools have been developed in the last decade for studying mean-field interacting jump processes and their limits under the assumption of detailed balance. To highlight only a few:
[
EFLS16
]
studied mean-field limits for measure-dependent jump processes;
[
Erb16
]
proved the convergence of the spatially-homogeneous Kac-process to the Boltzmann equation;
[
Sch19
]
investigated the macroscopic limit of Becker-Döring models;
[
KJZ19
]
showed hydrodynamic limits for zero-range and exclusion processes;
[
MM20
]
discussed convergence and higher-order approximations for chemical reaction networks, an approach that was subsequently used in the setting of discretized reaction-diffusion equations in
[
MSW22
]
.

In this work, we extend and apply these variational techniques to prove the mean-field limit for population dynamics over arbitrary compact Polish spaces, with bounded measurable parameters
m,c
satisfying a detailed balance condition. In addition, we establish entropic propagation of chaos, which controls the discrepancy between the microscopic and macroscopic models in a precise sense. To the authors’ knowledge, this is the first convergence result under such general assumptions.

To do so, we first introduce a new generalized gradient structure and rigorous variational formulation for the forward Kolmogorov equation (FKE) corresponding to the BPDL model, where the FKE describes the evolution of the law of the measure-valued process. Our formulation incorporates not only the equation itself but tracks the birth and death fluxes as well. This extends the generalized gradient-flow framework of
[
PRST22
]
due to the unboundedness of the underlying jump kernel, and the positivity of the fluxes.

We then show convergence of these generalized gradient structures under a mean-field scaling and the large-population limit in the sense of Energy Dissipation Principles (EDPs) (see
[
LMPR17
]
). The limiting gradient flow is the Liouville equation corresponding to the mean-field equation, namely a transport equation that describes the evolution of the law of a process that follows deterministic dynamics described by (
1.1
) but for possibly random initial conditions. This connection between the Liouville equation and the mean-field equation is made rigorous with the help of a modification of the superposition principle of
[
AC08
]
.

In particular, we deduce that the laws determined by the FKE equation concentrate around the solution of the mean-field equation (
1.1
), which due to the convergence of the associated free energies translates into an entropic propagation of chaos result, see Theorem
1.10
.

Outline

The rest of this section is devoted to giving a brief overview of our setting and presenting the main results. In Section
2
the mean-field equation and corresponding gradient structure are introduced. We repeat this process in Sections
3
and
4
for the forward Kolmogorov equation and the Liouville equation respectively, with the proof of a modified superposition principle delegated to Appendix
B
. Finally, in Section
5
, we establish the EDP-convergence of the gradient structures, and prove both the convergence to the mean-field limit and the propagation of chaos.

1.1.
Measure-valued population dynamics and mean-field limits

We consider the forward Kolmogorov equation that corresponds to a generalized version of the BPDL model. In its classical form, the Bolker-Pacala model is a purely spatially-structured microscopic model for a population of plants involving the birth, dispersal, and either natural death or death by competition for resources and can be modeled as a jump process in the space of positive measures over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, in certain models of adaptive evolution it is the mutation of traits that play a role, instead of spatial evolution (see
[
LD00
,
CFM06
,
CFM08
]
). Moreover, if one wants to model multiple interacting species or marked configuration spaces, more general spaces than
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are needed
[
KLU99
,
FKK21
]
).

Therefore, let the
trait
space be an arbitrary Polish space, denoted henceforth as
\mathcal{T}
. We model the BPDL-dynamics at any time
t
as an interacting particle system with particles
A_{t}^{1},\dots,A_{t}^{N_{t}}\in\mathcal{T}
at positions
X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N_{t}}\in\mathcal{T}
, where the number of particles
N_{t}
at time
t
is not fixed since particles can be removed from and added to the system.

Moreover, let
b\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
,
d,c\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T})
be non-negative measurable functions,
n>0
a positive parameter, and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\mathcal{T})
a non-negative reference measure such that

\int_{\mathcal{T}}d(x,y)\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}y)=1,\qquad\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathcal{T}.

Then the BPDL-dynamics can be described as follows:

•

Each particle located at a position
x\in\mathcal{T}
has two exponential clocks: a
seed
clock with rate
b(x)
and a
death
clock with rate
\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{t}}c(x,X_{t}^{i})
.

•

If the death clocks rings, the particle is deleted.

•

If the seed clock rings, a new particle is added at position
y\in\mathcal{T}
with probability
d(x,y)\gamma(\mathrm{d}y)
.

Alternatively, we can describe these dynamics in the form of reacting particles. Namely, setting
m(x,y):=b(x)d(x,y)
, then with a little of abuse of notation we have

(1.2)

\displaystyle A^{i}_{t}

\displaystyle\to A^{i}_{t}+A^{N_{t}+1}_{t}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{with rate}

\displaystyle m\left(X_{t}^{i},X_{t}^{N_{1}+1}\right)\gamma\left(X_{t}^{N_{1}+1}\right),

\displaystyle A^{i}_{t}+A^{j}_{t}

\displaystyle\to A^{j}_{t}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{with rate}

\displaystyle n^{-1}c\left(X^{i}_{t},X^{j}_{t}\right).

We will refer to
m
as the
mutation kernel
, and
c
as the
competition
kernel. The parameter
n>0
is called the
system size
, in the sense that that the scaling
n^{-1}c
guarantees that if the amount of particles in the system is of the order of
n
, the total rate of created or deleted particles is of the same order.

Instead of looking at the individual positions of the particles, it is common to only consider the measure-valued process
\nu_{t}
determined by the rescaled empirical measure

\nu_{t}^{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N(t)}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}.

Here,
\nu_{t}\in\Gamma:=\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
with
\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
the space of finite non-negative measures. The infinitesimal generator
Q_{n}
of this process is given for all
F\in C_{c}(\Gamma)
by

\qquad(Q_{n}F)(\nu)=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\left(F\left(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}\right)-F(\nu)\right)\,\kappa^{+}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x)+n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\left(F\left(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}\right)-F(\nu)\right)\,\kappa^{-}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x),

where
\kappa^{\pm}[\nu]\in\Gamma
are the measure-dependent birth/death-kernels

\kappa^{+}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x):=\left(\int_{y\in\mathcal{T}}m(y,x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad\kappa^{-}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x):=\left(\int_{y\in\mathcal{T}}c(x,y)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}x).

The law of the process is now given by the corresponding forward Kolmogorov equation

(
\sf FKE_{n}
)

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}=Q_{n}^{*}\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n},\qquad\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma).

Depending on the setting, this formulation can be made rigorous in various ways: for example via an analytical approach on configuration spaces as done in
[
FKK09
]
, which in fact models infinite configurations of particles over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or via martingale techniques with
\mathcal{T}
a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma=\mathscr{L}^{d}|_{\mathcal{T}}
(see
[
FM04
]
). Moreover, in the latter, under the assumption of continuous, bounded, and integrable mutation/competition kernels, it is also shown that the process converges in the large-population limit
n\to\infty
to the mean-field equation (
1.1
), which can be rewritten as

(
\sf MF
)

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=\kappa^{+}[\nu_{t}]-\kappa^{-}[\nu_{t}],\qquad\nu_{t}\in\Gamma.

While different choices of scalings are possible, the mean-field equation describes the macroscopic properties of the measure-valued process when the population is large. An alternative way is to study the evolution of the moments, which form a hierarchy similar to the BBGKY-hierarchy of correlation functions, and under the so-called Vlasov scaling the first moment or correlation function converges to (

MF

). For the case of infinite configurations over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
this has been established, see
[
FKK10
]
, and both propagation of chaos in the Vlasov limit and the sub-Poissonian property have been established as well
[
FKKK15
]
.

In this work, we do not consider the measure-valued process itself, but take the forward Kolmogorov equation (

FKE
n

) as a starting point, and show convergence to the mean-field equation in the sense that
\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}\to\delta_{\nu_{t}}
narrowly on
\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
under suitable initial conditions. Throughout we assume the following:

Assumption 1.1
.

The trait space
\mathcal{T}
is a compact Polish space, and moreover

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\in\Gamma\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{(reference measure with finite mass)}

\displaystyle m,c

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}_{b}^{+}(\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T})\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{(bounded rates)}

\displaystyle c(x,x)

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathcal{T}\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{(no natural death)}

\displaystyle m(y,x)

\displaystyle=c(x,y)\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{ for all }x,y\in\mathcal{T}\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{(detailed balance)}

Henceforth we equip the space
\Gamma
with the narrow topology. Moreover, the assumption of no natural death means that particles can only be deleted due to competition with other particles. Together with the detailed balance condition this guarantees that the jump kernel is reversible with respect to an invariant measure
\Pi_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
, which is obtained as a push-forward of the Poisson measure
\pi_{n}
with

\mathcal{P}\left(\coprod_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{T}^{N}\right)\ni\pi_{n}:=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\gamma^{\otimes N}.

This allows us to write the forward Kolmogorov equation as a gradient flow of the relative entropy with respect to
\Pi_{n}
, and equip it with a corresponding variational structure, see Theorem
1.6
.

In light of similar results in
[
EFLS16
,
MM20
]
for mean-field jump processes on finite spaces and finite chemical reaction networks, one expects (

FKE
n

) to converge to the following
Liouville equation

(
\sf Li
)

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}+\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma}\left(\mathsf{P}_{t}\,\bigl(\kappa^{+}[\nu]-\kappa^{-}[\nu]\bigr)\right)=0,\qquad\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma).

It is a transport equation that can be interpreted as the lifting of mean-field dynamics in
\Gamma
to evolutions in
\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
, and describes the evolution of the law of random measures
\nu_{t}
that all satisfy (

MF

). In particular, if
\nu_{t}
a solution of (

MF

) then
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=\delta_{\nu_{t}}
is itself a solution of (

Li

).

It turns out that in our general setting this convergence holds as well, as will be stated in Theorem
1.9
. Letting
V[\nu]=\kappa^{+}[\nu]-\kappa^{-}[\nu]
, we can therefore represent part of our results in Figure
1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eqref{eq:Forward Kolmogorov}\quad\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}=Q_{n}^{*}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}\;}

(

Li

)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}+\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma}\,(\mathsf{P}_{t}V[\nu])=0,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n},\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma),}

(

MF

)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=V[\nu_{t}],}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{t}\in\Gamma:=\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathcal{T}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n\to\infty}

Figure 1.
Convergence in the large-population limit

This convergence is a direct consequence of the convergence of the associated gradient structures, which we will describe below.

1.2.
Gradient-flow formulation

Our first main result concerns the variational formulation of the equations (

FKE
n

), (

MF

), (

Li

) and their specific gradient structure. Various gradient-flow formulations exist for jump processes, mean-field jump processes, and chemical reaction networks
[
EFLS16
,
Erb16
,
KJZ19
,
MM20
,
PRST22
]
.

In these works a common starting point is to describe the relation between
\rho_{t}
, representing either laws of some process or mean-field limits and generalized
fluxes

j_{t}
in the form of an abstract
continuity equation
. For example, in the case of independent particles following a common jump process over a graph,
\rho_{t}
corresponds to the number of particles on a node at time
t
, and a choice of flux
j_{t}
can be the so-called
net
flux
j_{t}
, which is related to the number of particles going through an edge.

However, we propose a slightly different structure, namely one that tracks the effective
mass fluxes
for both creation (arising from mutation) and annihilation (arising from competition) separately. The use of mass fluxes instead of usual particle fluxes ensures that in our convergence results as
n\to\infty
we have both convergences of laws and fluxes (see Theorem
1.8
).

Moreover, separating the effects of creation and annihilation (henceforth simply referred to as birth and death) instead of their combined contribution allows us to incorporate more information in our variational formulation. The downside is that we are forced to work with
positive fluxes
, while the framework in the aforementioned examples involves either quadratic or generalized structures for signed net fluxes. In this sense we are closer to the variational representations stemming from large deviations, involving so-called one-way or unidirectional fluxes, see for example
[
MPR14
,
PR19
,
BBBO21
,
PS22
]
. Indeed, our structure is motivated by large deviation theory, as we will discuss briefly in Appendix
A
.

In all three cases, i.e. for (

FKE
n

), (

MF

) and (

Li

), our proposed structure is similar to the classical notion of a gradient flow in the sense that they all satisfy an abstract Energy-Dissipation Balance. Since we will repeat the same concept three times on different levels and for different spaces, let us make the general and abstract concepts clear:

Formal Definition 1.2
.

Given a
free energy
functional
\mathcal{F}(\rho)
, a
dissipation potential

\mathcal{R}(\rho,j)
, a
Fisher information
functional
\mathcal{D}(\rho)
, and a linear operator
B
with dual
B^{*}
, we consider pairs of curves
(\rho,j)
satisfying the continuity equation

(
\mathsf{CE}
)

\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+B^{*}j_{t}=0,\quad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$,}

and define the
EDP-functional

\mathcal{I}(\rho,j):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}(\rho_{t},j_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}(\rho_{T})-\mathcal{F}(\rho_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}(\rho_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Moreover, a
gradient-flow solution
is a pair
(\hat{\rho},\hat{\jmath})
satisfying (

CE

) with
I(\hat{\rho},\hat{\jmath})=0
.

Throughout we require the non-negativity of
\mathcal{I}
. For a deeper look at the mathematical basis of this sort of setting, especially for generalized gradient systems incorporating net fluxes, see
[
PRST22
]
.

In all three examples the generalized fluxes
j
consist of two parts:
j^{+}
and
j^{-}
, corresponding to birth and death. The continuity equations depend on the setting and are summarized in Table
1
, with
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}
as the space of non-negative Radon measures.

Remark 1.3
.

Note that the gradient-flow solution
(\hat{\rho},\hat{\jmath})
is the null-minimizer of
\mathcal{I}
, and satisfies the
energy-dissipation balance

\mathcal{F}(\hat{\rho}_{T})+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}(\hat{\rho}_{t},\hat{\jmath}_{t})+\mathcal{D}(\hat{\rho}_{t})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathcal{F}(\hat{\rho}_{0}).

Moreover, for small
T\ll 1
one would expect

I\approx\mathcal{R}(\hat{\rho},\hat{\jmath})+\langle\hat{\jmath},B\,\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{F}\rangle+\mathcal{D}(\hat{\rho}).

In light of the generalized gradient-flow framework of
[
PRST22
]
and the relation to minimizing movement schemes, a formal minimization procedure provides the gradient-flow solution

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}+B^{*}\hat{\jmath}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{\jmath}

\displaystyle=(\,\partial_{2}\mathcal{R}^{*})(\hat{\rho},-B\,\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{F}),

and that along the solution

(1.3)

\mathcal{D}(\hat{\rho})=\mathcal{R}^{*}(\hat{\rho},-B\,\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{F}).

where
\mathcal{R}^{*}(\rho,w)
is the dual of the dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}
. Finally, note that along the gradient-flow solution the free energy
\mathcal{F}
is non-increasing, i.e.
\mathcal{F}
is a Lyapunov functional.

These (in)equalities indeed hold in our setting. See also Appendix
A
, where we compare the relation to generalized gradient flows for net fluxes, which follow from the above after a contraction argument, and the connection to the reversibility of the underlying process.

\mathsf{CE}

\rho

j=(j^{+},j^{-})

BF

(

MF

)

(

⁢
C
E

)

\nu\in\Gamma

(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\Gamma^{2}

(F,-F)

(

FKE
n

)

(

CE
n

)

\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)

(\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})^{2}

(\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F,\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F)
(
1.4
)

(

Li

)

(

CE
∞

)

\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)

(\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})^{2}

(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F,-\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)
(
1.5
)

Table 1.
Continuity equations

Let
H(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
be the Hellinger distance, see (
2.2
), and
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mu_{1}|\mu_{2})
the relative entropy of
\mu_{1}
with respect to
\mu_{2}
for two (possible infinite) locally finite Borel measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
:

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mu_{1}|\mu_{2}):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2},&&\mbox{if $\mu_{1}\ll\mu_{2}$,}\\
&+\infty,\qquad&&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{aligned}\right.

where

\phi(s)=s\log s-s+1.

With the full technical details contained in Theorems
2.7
,
3.8
and
4.7
, we then have the following triple of results below,

Theorem 1.4

(Mean-field, cf. Theorem
2.7
)
.

Consider triples
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
, with
\nu_{t},\lambda_{t}^{\pm}\in\Gamma
, satisfying the mean-field continuity equation

(
\mathscr{CE}
)

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=\lambda_{t}^{+}-\lambda^{-}_{t}.

Define the dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}_{MF}
, free energy
\mathcal{F}_{MF}
and Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{MF}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{+}|\theta_{\nu})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{-}|\theta_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu)

\displaystyle:=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &2H^{2}(\kappa^{+}[\nu],\kappa^{-}[\nu])\qquad&&\mbox{if $\nu\ll\gamma$,}\\
&+\infty,\qquad&&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{aligned}\right.

where
\theta_{\nu}
is the geometric mean of the expected birth and death fluxes, i.e.

\theta_{\nu}:=\sqrt{\kappa^{+}[\nu]\kappa^{-}[\nu]}.

Then the corresponding EDP-functional
I_{MF}
given by

\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

is non-negative, and for any
\nu_{0}
with
\mathcal{F}(\nu_{0})<\infty
a unique gradient-flow solution
(\hat{\nu},\hat{\lambda}^{+},\hat{\lambda}^{-})
exists, with
\hat{\nu}_{t}
equal to the unique strong solution to (

MF

) and
\hat{\lambda}_{t}^{\pm}=\kappa^{\pm}[\hat{\nu}_{t}]
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
.

As mentioned, although treating birth and death separately provides us with additional information, this prohibits the use of some of the previous works for gradient structures because of the positivity of the fluxes. However, there is still a strong connection to the variational formulations for jump processes arising from the large deviations of fluxes as seen in
[
PR19
]
and
[
BBBO21
]
, see for example Appendix
A
on the equivalence of the EDP-functional to the expected rate functional.

Remark 1.5
.

It is straightforward to verify that if
\mathrm{d}\nu=u\mathrm{d}\gamma

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{MF}(\nu,\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{F}_{MF},-\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{F}_{MF})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}1_{u(x)>0}c(x,y)\left(\sqrt{u(x)}-1\right)^{2}\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}c(x,y)\left(\sqrt{u(x)}-1\right)^{2}\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y),

and hence it is not directly clear that the relation (
1.3
) holds. However, as will be shown for Theorem
2.7
, at least along the solution
\hat{\nu}_{t}
the equivalence holds for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

Theorem 1.6

(Forward Kolmogorov, cf. Theorem
3.8
)
.

Consider triples
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
, with
\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
and
J_{t}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
, satisfying the continuity equation

(
\mathsf{CE}_{n}
)

\displaystyle\langle F,\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}\rangle=\langle\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F,\mathsf{J}_{t}^{+}\rangle+\langle\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F,\mathsf{J}_{t}^{-}\rangle,\qquad\forall F\in C_{c}(\Gamma),

where

(1.4)

(\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}F)(\nu,x):=n\left(F(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\right).

Define the
n
-dependent Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{n}
as stated in Definition
3.4
, free energy

\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}):=\frac{1}{2n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi_{n}),

and dissipation potential

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{-}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,-}),

where, with a little abuse of notation (see (
3.14
)),

\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,\pm}(\nu,x):=\sqrt{\Big(\mathsf{P}(\nu)\kappa^{\pm}[\nu]\Big)\left(\mathsf{P}(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})\kappa^{\mp}[\nu\mp\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}]\right)}.

Then the corresponding EDP-functional
I_{n}
given by

\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

is non-negative, and for any
\mathsf{P}_{0}
with
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
a unique gradient-flow solution
(\hat{\mathsf{P}},\hat{\mathsf{J}}^{\pm})
exists, with
\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}
equal to a weak solution to (

FKE
n

) and
\hat{\mathsf{J}}_{t}^{\pm}=\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
.

Similar to the mean-field case, the dissipation potential consists of relative entropies with respect to geometric averages, now of forward and backward rates along a transition
\nu\to\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}
.
Moreover, note that in contrast to the framework of
[
PRST22
]
, we employ fluxes
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
that are not finite measures. This is due to the unboundedness of
\kappa_{\nu}
as the mass of
\nu
grows, which implies that the underlying jump kernel over
\Gamma
is itself unbounded as well, see Section
3
.

For the Liouville equation, let us define
\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
as the space of compactly supported smooth cylinder functions of the form

F(\nu)=g\left(\langle 1,\nu\rangle,\langle f_{1},\nu\rangle,\dots,\langle f_{m},\nu\rangle\right),\qquad g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),\;m\in\mathbb{N},

where
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
, and
\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}
is the distributional gradient defined by

(1.5)

\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}\,F(\nu,x)=(\nabla g)\left(\langle 1,\nu\rangle,\langle f_{1},\nu\rangle,\dots,\langle f_{m},\nu\rangle\right)\cdot(1,f_{1}(x),\dots,f_{m}(x))^{\top}.

Theorem 1.7

(Liouville, cf. Theorem
4.7
)
.

Consider triples
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
, with
\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
,
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
, satisfying the continuity equation

(
\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
)

\langle F,\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}\rangle=\langle\mathsf{grad}_{\Gamma}F,\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{grad}_{\Gamma}F,\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t}\rangle,\quad\forall F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma).

Define the Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
as stated in Definition
4.4
, free energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P},

and dissipation potential

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\infty})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{-}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\infty}),\qquad\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x):=\theta_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

Then the corresponding EDP-functional
I_{\infty}
given by

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

is non-negative, and for any
\mathsf{P}_{0}
with
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\nu_{0})<\infty
a unique gradient-flow solution
(\hat{\mathsf{P}},\hat{\mathsf{J}}^{\pm})
exists, with
\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}
a weak solution to (

Li

) and
\hat{\mathsf{J}}_{t}^{\pm}=\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
.

Finally, for any
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
such that
I_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})<\infty
, there exists (with a little abuse of notation) a Borel probability measure
\Omega
over curves satisfying the mean-field continuity equation (

⁢
C
E

) such that for all
t
the time marginals
(e_{t})_{\#}\Omega
are equal to
\mathsf{P}_{t}
, and

(1.6)

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\int\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\,\mathrm{d}\Omega.

The statement of (
1.6
) is the aforementioned superposition principle, which is a modified version of the superposition principle
[
AT14
]
in metric measure spaces, and the ones used in
[
EFLS16
]
,
[
Erb16
]
. It allows one to essentially jump back and forth between the Liouville equation and the mean-field dynamics, and in particular, provides us with the non-negativity of
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
and uniqueness of gradient-flow solutions.

1.3.
Convergence results

Our final and most important result is that the above gradient structures converge in the sense of
EDP-convergence
(e.g. see
[
LMPR17
,
PS22
]
), a generalization of the evolutionary
\Gamma
-convergence approach stated by
[
SS04
,
Ser11
]
and expanded on in
[
Mie16
]
, which implies convergence of the gradient-flow solutions and their free energies.

We say that a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
converges to some
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}
if for all
t\in[0,T]
the probability measures
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}
converge narrowly to
\mathsf{P}_{t}
in
\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
, and
\mathsf{J}^{n,\pm}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t
converge vaguely to
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t
in
\mathcal{M}_{loc}([0,T]\times\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
. Again postponing technicalities, see Theorem
5.1
, we have the following lower semi-continuity and compactness result:

Theorem 1.8

(cf. Theorem
5.1
)
.

The sequence of free energies
\mathcal{F}_{n}

\varGamma
-converges to
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
.

Moreover, the sequence of Fisher-information functionals and dissipation potentials are all sequentially lower semicontinuous. In particular, for any sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
converging to a
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})\to\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})
as well, we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\geq\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}).

Finally, for any sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})<\infty,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})<\infty,

there exists a subsequence converging to some
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
.

Here the notion of EDP-convergence or evolutionary
\varGamma
-convergence (where the
\varGamma
is not to be confused with our space of positive measures
\Gamma
) relates to the
\varGamma
-convergence of the free energies
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and suitable liminf-estimates for the dissipation potentials and Fisher-information functionals (or local slopes in a metric setting).

In certain applications or for certain notions of convergence (e.g. see
[
MMP21
]
) one also establishes
\varGamma
-convergence for the total dissipation
\mathcal{R}_{n}+\mathcal{D}_{n}
when written as functionals over
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}(\Gamma))
. Moreover,
\varGamma
-convergence of the functionals
\mathcal{I}_{n}
over such path-spaces are related to the large deviations of the underlying process
[
Kra19
]
, as we briefly discuss in Appendix
A
. In our framework this would require that for every
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
, we can find a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
that converges to
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
and satisfies the limsup-estimate

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\leq\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}).

However, in this paper we restrict ourselves only to the liminf-estimates, which is sufficient to obtain convergence of the solutions, an approach also taken in
[
EFLS16
,
Erb16
,
MM20
]
. Namely, by a lower semicontinuity and compactness argument, Theorem
1.8
implies the convergence of both the solutions and the free energies
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, if the initial data are well prepared.

Theorem 1.9

(cf. Theorem
5.3
)
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n}\to\mathsf{P}
with
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})\to\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})
as well. Then for the sequence
\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{n}
of gradient-flow solutions to (

FKE
n

), and
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
the gradient-flow solution to (

Li

), we have that for all
t\in[0,T]

\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}^{n}\to\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}\mbox{\; narrowly,\hskip 10.22217ptand}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{n}_{t})=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}).

In particular, if
\mathsf{P}_{0}=\delta_{\hat{\nu}_{0}}
and
\hat{\nu}_{t}
is the solution to the mean-field problem (

MF

), then for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}^{n}\to\delta_{\hat{\nu}_{t}}\mbox{\; narrowly,\hskip 10.22217ptand}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{n}_{t}|\Pi_{n})=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\hat{\nu}_{t}|\gamma).

The second half of Theorem
1.9
, on the concentration around mean-field solutions and convergence of entropies, follows directly from the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
and uniqueness.

For interacting particle systems where the number of particles is fixed at
n\in\mathbb{N}
the narrow convergence
\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}^{n}\to\delta_{\hat{\nu}_{t}}
is equivalent to propagation of chaos in the sense of Snitzman
[
Szn91
]
, and would imply narrow convergence of the
k
-particle marginals at time
t
to
\nu_{t}^{\otimes k}
. However, in our setting, this implies convergence of the
k
-correlation functions, see
[
BGSRS20
]
.

Moreover, the convergence of the free energies
\mathcal{F}_{n}
implies the stronger notion of entropic propagation of chaos if the initial condition is sufficiently regular.

Theorem 1.10

(cf. Theorem
5.4
)
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{P}^{n}_{0}\to\delta_{\hat{\nu}_{0}}
with
C^{-1}\leq\mathrm{d}\hat{\nu}_{0}/\mathrm{d}\gamma\leq C
for some
C>0
. If

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{0}^{n}|\Pi_{n,\hat{\nu}_{0}})=0,

then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{t}^{n}|\Pi_{n,\hat{\nu}_{t}})=0,\qquad\mbox{ for all }t\geq 0,

where
\Pi_{n,\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
stems from the Poisson measure
\pi_{n,\nu}
with intensity measure
\nu
, i.e.

\pi_{n,\nu}:=\frac{1}{e^{n\nu(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\nu^{\otimes N}.

To the authors’ knowledge, this is the first entropic propagation of chaos result for bounded competition kernels over compact Polish spaces, under the assumption of detailed balance.

Comments

We have given an overview of the generalized gradient structures that we introduced for the forward Kolmogorov equation of our underlying interacting particle system and eluded to how this sequence of structures converges to a gradient structure induced by the mean-field limit. Throughout, we assumed bounded measurable rates
m,c
over a compact Polish space
\mathcal{T}
satisfying the detailed balance condition
m(x,y)=c(x,y)
and
c(x,x)=0
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{T}
, and we would like to briefly touch on possible relaxations of these assumptions.

First, for the limit inferior in Theorem
5.1
, there is a technical issue concerning the possible non-continuity of the competition kernel
c
, which we resolve by an approximation argument from large deviation theory
[
HHMT20
]
, see Appendix
C
. This argument can be straightforwardly extended to unbounded rates
m
and
c
under certain exponential integrability estimates with respect to the reference measure
\gamma
. However, the uniqueness of solutions and well-posed of variational formulations would be less clear.

Moreover, it should be noted that although for brevity and clarity we chose
\mathcal{T}
to be compact, many of the listed results carry over to the case of
\mathcal{T}
Polish with finite
\gamma
, under suitable choices of topologies and by bootstrapping from the tightness of
\gamma
. For
\sigma
-finite
\gamma
, this is not necessarily the case and would depend strongly on newly constructed estimates on the propagation of tightness.

A more fundamental restriction is the detailed balance assumption, which is necessary to phrase the variational structures in terms of generalized gradient systems and the evolution in terms of a gradient flow. However, there exist possible extensions and decompositions of variational structures for jump processes that do not assume detailed balance or even complex balance, see for example
[
KJZ18
]
for an overview. Therefore, in future work, the authors plan to generalize the variational methods outlined here to more general evolutions.

4.4

2.
Mean-field system

In this section, we will discuss the gradient-flow formulation of the mean-field equation under the detailed balance condition. Let us first make precise the context of Theorem
1.4
, and embed it within the more general statement of Theorem
2.7
below.

Recall that the trait space
\mathcal{T}
is a compact Polish space, and
\Gamma:=\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is the space of finite non-negative measures over
\mathcal{T}
equipped with the narrow topology. Fix a reference measure
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and rates
m,c
satisfying Assumption
1.1
, i.e.
m,c\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T})
with
m(x,y)=c(y,x)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{T}
, and
c(x,x)=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{T}
. The mean-field equation then reads

(
\sf MF
)

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=\kappa^{+}[\nu_{t}]-\kappa^{-}[\nu_{t}],

with measure-dependent birth and death kernels
\kappa^{\pm}:\Gamma\to\Gamma
given by

(2.1)

\kappa^{+}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x):=\int_{y\in\mathcal{T}}c(x,y)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y),\qquad\kappa^{-}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x):=\int_{y\in\mathcal{T}}c(x,y)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y).

Routinely, we will also adopt the shorthand notation
\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}:=\kappa^{\pm}[\nu]
. Now, setting
c_{\nu}(x):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}c(x,y)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)
, it is clear that that
\kappa^{+}_{\nu}=c_{\nu}\gamma
,
\kappa^{-}_{\nu}=c_{\nu}\nu
, and the dynamics simplify to

\partial_{t}\nu(\mathrm{d}x)=c_{\nu}(x)(\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)-\nu(\mathrm{d}x)).

Strong solutions to (

MF

) in either total variation or appropriate
L^{1}
spaces follow straightforwardly via classical methods, see Section
2.2
.

The total variation norm
\|\cdot\|_{TV}
on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{T})
is defined as

\|\mu\|_{TV}:=\sup\left\{\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu:f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T}),\,\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1\right\},\qquad\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{T}),

and the squared Hellinger distance
H^{2}
is given by

(2.2)

H^{2}(\nu,\eta):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\sigma}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\sigma}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma,

with
\sigma
a measure dominating both
\mu
and
\nu
. Note that the definition (
2.2
) is independent of the choice for the dominating measure
\sigma
, and
\sigma=\nu+\eta
is always admissible.

Moreover, recall the entropy function
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and its Legendre dual
\phi^{*}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\phi(s):=s\log s-s+1,\qquad\phi^{*}(z):=e^{z}-1,

and the relative entropy of
\nu
with respect to
\mu
as

(2.3)

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\mu):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu,&&\quad\mbox{if $\nu\ll\mu$},\\
&+\infty&&\quad\mbox{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

We will consider curves satisfying the continuity equation

(
\mathscr{CE}
)

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=\lambda_{t}^{+}-\lambda_{t}^{-},

in an appropriately weak sense.

Definition 2.1

(Mean-field continuity equation)
.

A triple
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
satisfies the mean-field continuity equation
\mathscr{CE}
if

(1)

the curve
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\nu_{t}\in\Gamma
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{TV}
,

(2)

the Borel family
(\lambda_{t}^{\pm})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\Gamma
satisfies
\int_{0}^{T}\|\lambda_{t}^{\pm}\|_{TV}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
,

(3)

for every
s,t\in[0,T]
and all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})

\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}^{+}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}^{-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mbox{for all $s,t$ with $0\leq s,t\leq T$.}

We will refer to
\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}=\lambda^{+}-\lambda^{-}
as the
net flux
.

Remark 2.2
.

When seen as approximations of particle systems the birth/death fluxes
\lambda^{\pm}_{t}
represent the
observed
amount of mass being created/annihilated around a certain point, and
\nu_{t}
represents the density of the particles, while
\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}
correspond to the
expected
birth and death fluxes of the BPDL model.

Remark 2.3

(Time-regularity)
.

As we will see in Lemma
2.11
, if there exist a common dominating measure for
\{\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
then the continuity equation holds in a strong sense:
\nu_{t}
is an a.e. differentiable map from
[0,T]
to
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV})
and

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=\lambda_{t}^{+}-\lambda_{t}^{-},\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\theta_{\nu}
be the geometric average of
\kappa_{\nu}^{+}
and
\kappa_{\nu}^{-}
, i.e.

\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}:=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma}\frac{\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{-}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma}}\mathrm{d}\sigma,

for any dominating measure
\sigma
. We define the following objects:

•

The dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}_{MF}:\Gamma^{3}\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{+}|\theta_{\nu})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{-}|\theta_{\nu}),

and the dual dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}^{*}_{MF}:\Gamma\times\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{X})^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{R}^{*}_{MF}(\nu,w^{+},w^{-}):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}(e^{w^{+}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}+\int_{\mathcal{T}}(e^{w^{-}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}.

•

The free energy
\mathcal{F}_{MF}:\Gamma\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu):=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma),

and Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{MF}:\Gamma\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &2H^{2}(\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\kappa_{\nu}^{-}),&&\qquad\mbox{if $\nu\ll\gamma,$}\\
&+\infty,&&\qquad\mbox{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

•

The
EDP-functional

\mathcal{I}_{MF}:\mathscr{CE}\to[0,+\infty]
for all curves with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty

(2.4)

\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}(\nu_{T})-\mathcal{F}(\nu_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Remark 2.5
.

Since
\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})<\infty
by Lemma
2.9
all objects above are well-defined, and it is straightforward to verify via the dual representation of the entropy that
\mathcal{R}_{MF},\mathcal{R}^{*}_{MF}
are truly dual objects in the sense that

\mathcal{R}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\sup_{w^{\pm}\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})}\left\{\int_{\mathcal{T}}w^{+}\mathrm{d}\lambda^{+}+\int_{\mathcal{T}}w^{-}\mathrm{d}\lambda^{-}-\mathcal{R}^{*}(\nu,w^{+},w^{-})\right\},

and vice versa.

Remark 2.6
.

If
\nu\ll\gamma
with
\mathrm{d}\nu=u\mathrm{d}\gamma
, note that
\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}=c_{\nu}\sqrt{u}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma
, and that the Fisher information simplifies to

\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{T}}c_{\nu}\left(\sqrt{u}-1\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma.

We are now able to fully state the variational characterization of strong solutions to the mean-field equation (

MF

).

Theorem 2.7
.

For any
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\geq 0
and

\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})=0\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} \quad&\mbox{$\nu_{t}$ is the unique strong solution to \eqref{eq:mf}},\quad\\
\quad\lambda^{\pm}_{t}&=(\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu_{t}})\quad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$.}\quad\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover, whenever
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
and
\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})<\infty
the chain rule for
\mathcal{F}_{MF}
holds:
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{t})
is absolutely continuous and

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{t})=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}(\lambda^{+}_{t}-\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

The proof of Theorem
2.7
is postponed to Section
2.3
, where we establish the main technical ingredient, namely the chain rule for the entropy functional.

Remark 2.8
.

The non-negativity of
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
and the fact that null-minimizers are solutions to (

MF

) is related to the formal equivalence

\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}(\nu_{t},\lambda_{t}^{+},\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
\mathcal{L}
is the so-called
Lagrangian
given by

\mathcal{L}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{+}|\kappa_{\nu}^{+})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{+}|\kappa_{\nu}^{-}).

Note that
\mathcal{L}
is non-negative and zero if only if
\lambda^{\pm}=\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}
.
Although we do not prove the full equivalence in this work, it does play a role in the intuition and motivation behind the EDP-functional
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
with the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
stemming from a large deviation perspective, as seen in Appendix
A
.

2.1.
A priori estimates

In this section, we will collect some elementary estimates and results that are either necessary for the well-posedness of the mean-field equation and the corresponding gradient structure, or necessary to do the same for the Liouville equation in Section
4
.

Let
\Psi^{*}
be given as

(2.5)

\Psi^{*}(z):=2(\cosh(z)-1)=e^{z}+e^{-z}-2,

and its dual
\Psi:=(\Psi^{*})^{*}

(2.6)

\Psi(s)=s\log\left(\frac{s+\sqrt{s^{2}+4}}{2}\right)-\sqrt{s^{2}+4}+2

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
M:=\|c\|_{\infty}(1+\gamma(\mathcal{T}))
. Then the following estimates hold:

(i)

The measures
\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}
and
\theta_{\nu}
are finite:

(2.7)

\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2}),

and

(2.8)

\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})

(ii)

For any birth/death fluxes
\lambda^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, net flux
\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}=\lambda^{+}-\lambda-
, and
w^{\pm},w\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{T})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}}|w^{\pm}|\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\pm}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{\pm}|\theta_{\nu})+\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(w)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}+\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T}),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}}|w|\,\mathrm{d}|\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})+\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(w)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu},

(iii)

For any birth/death fluxes
\lambda^{\pm}\in\Gamma
,

(2.9)

\phi\left(\frac{\lambda^{\pm}(\mathcal{T})}{M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})}\vee 1\right)M\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})

Remark 2.10
.

Although the estimate for
\theta_{\nu}
can be made more precise, namely

\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq\|c\|_{\infty}\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{1/2}\nu(\mathcal{T})^{3/2},

we will not require it for our results.

Proof.

(i)
With
\theta_{\nu}:=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{+}/\mathrm{d}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{-}/\mathrm{d}\sigma}\,\sigma
for any dominating measure
\sigma
we have by Hölder’s inequality

\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq\sqrt{\kappa_{\nu}^{+}(\mathcal{T})\kappa_{\nu}^{-}(\mathcal{T})}.

Note that
\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq\|c\|_{\infty}\gamma(\mathcal{T})\nu(\mathcal{T})
, and
\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq\|c\|_{\infty}\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2}
, which provides (
2.7
). Since
z\leq 1+z^{2}
for all
z\geq 0
(
2.8
) follows directly.

(ii)
First, suppose that
w\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})
. Using the elementary inequality
e^{|a|}\leq e^{a}+e^{-a}
we derive by duality of the entropy

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}}|w|\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\pm}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{\pm}|\theta_{\nu})+\int_{\mathcal{T}}(e^{|w|}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{\pm}|\theta_{\nu})+\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(w)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}+\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T}).

Next, fix any measurable function
w\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{T})
and set its
k
-truncation
w_{k}:=\max\{\min\{w,k\},-k\}
. Since
\Psi^{*}
is even and monotone, by dominated convergence applied to the left-hand side and monotone convergence to the right-hand side, the inequality holds for
w
as well.

(iii)
Without loss of generality, suppose that
\mathcal{R}_{MF}
is finite. Set
a(\nu):=(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}
, and note that
0\leq a(\nu)\leq 1
. With
\tilde{\phi}(s):=\phi(s\vee 1)
the monotone relaxation of
\phi
, we then have the following chain of inequalities,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathcal{T}}\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathcal{T}}\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}(a(\nu)\lambda^{\pm})}{\mathrm{d}(a(\nu)\theta_{\nu})}\right)\mathrm{d}(a(\nu)\theta_{\nu})

\displaystyle\geq\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{a(\nu)\lambda^{\pm}(\mathcal{T})}{a(\nu)\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}\right)a(\nu)\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T}),

where the last inequality follows from Jensen’s inequality. By convexity of
\tilde{\phi}
and
\tilde{\phi}(0)=0
the latter expression is monotone in
\theta_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})
, and hence by (
2.8
) we find

\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{\lambda^{\pm}(\mathcal{T})}{M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})}\right)M\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}).

∎

We will briefly state the improvement of regularity in time of
\nu_{t}
if there exists a common dominating measure. The proof is similar to Corollary 4.14 of
[
PRST22
]
and therefore omitted here.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
and suppose that there exists a measure
\ell\in\Gamma
such that
\nu_{t},\lambda_{t}^{\pm}\ll\ell
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Then there exists an absolutely continuous and a.e. differentiable map
u:[0,T]\to L^{1}(\mathcal{T},\ell)
and maps
g^{\pm}:[0,T]\to L^{1}(\mathcal{T},\ell)
such that
u_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}/\mathrm{d}\ell
,
g_{t}^{\pm}=\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}^{\pm}/\mathrm{d}\ell
and

\partial_{t}u_{t}(x)=g_{t}^{+}(x)-g_{t}^{-}(x),\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

In particular, the continuity equation holds in the strong sense, namely that
\nu_{t}
is an a.e. differentiable map from
[0,T]
to
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV})
and

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}=\lambda_{t}^{+}-\lambda_{t}^{-},\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

Next, we will list two results that are either necessary for the chain rule in Section
3.3
or the superposition principle and well-posedness of the continuity equation in Section
4
.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any
0\leq a\leq 1
,
z\in\mathbb{R}

(2.10)

\Psi^{*}(az)\leq a^{2}\Psi^{*}(z).

Moreover, for any net flux
\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{T})
,
w\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{T})

(2.11)

\displaystyle\Psi\left(\frac{\|\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}\|_{TV}}{M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))}\right)M

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}).

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that
\Psi^{*}(z)/z^{2}
is monotone increasing for
z\geq 0
, from which the first statement follows.

Now, for the net flux, it is convenient to go through the dual representation. Set
a(\nu):=(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{-1}
. By duality, for any
w\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\geq a(\nu)\int_{\mathcal{T}}w(x)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}\big(a(\nu)w(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}.

However, by (
2.10
),

\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}\big(a(\nu)w(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}\leq\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}\big(w(x)\big)a(\nu)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}\leq M\Psi^{*}(\|w\|_{\infty}).

Taking the supremum over all
w\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})
in (
2.12
) we find (
2.11
).
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
be a countable and dense set of bounded continuous functions. Suppose
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})
is such that

(i)

the curve
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\nu_{t}\in\Gamma
is narrowly continuous

(ii)

(\lambda_{t}^{\pm})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\Gamma
is a Borel family with

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

(iii)

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}

\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}^{+}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}^{-}\right)\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mbox{for all $s,t$ with $0\leq s,t\leq T$.}

Then
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
, i.e. the triple satisfies the mean-field continuity equation.

Proof.

Since
\nu_{t}
is narrowly continuous its mass is uniformly bounded in time, hence let
C:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})
. By (
2.9
) and monotonicity of
\phi(\cdot\vee 1)
we have for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

\phi\left(\frac{\lambda_{t}^{\pm}(\mathcal{T})}{M(1+C^{2})}\vee 1\right)M\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t}),

and therefore by convexity of
\phi(\cdot\vee 1)

\int_{0}^{T}\lambda_{t}^{\pm}(\mathcal{T})<\infty.

Since the measures
\lambda_{t}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t\in\mathcal{M}^{+}([0,T]\times\Gamma)
are finite, by density of
f_{i}
in
C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
it is clear that for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})

\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}^{+}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}^{-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mbox{for all $s,t$ with $0\leq s,t\leq T$.}

By a monotone class argument this can be extended to all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})
and we derive that
\nu_{t}
is indeed TV-absolutely continuous and
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
.
∎

2.2.
Strong solutions

Strong solutions to (

MF

) exist and are unique, and we list the most important properties here. It should be noted that these arguments apply even without the detailed balance condition
m(x,y)=c(y,x)
and only require both
\|m\|_{\infty}
and
\|c\|_{\infty}<\infty
to be finite, but for simplicity, we will restrict ourselves to our framework. Moreover, in all results the time window
T>0
is arbitrary.

Definition 2.14
.

A
strong
solution to (

MF

) is any TV-absolutely continuous and a.e. differentiable mapping
\nu:[0,T]\to(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV})
satisfying

(2.13)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nu_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\nu_{t}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)-\kappa_{\nu_{t}}^{-}(\mathrm{d}x)

Recall that
\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)=\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu\rangle\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)
and
\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)=\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu\rangle\nu(\mathrm{d}x)
.

Remark 2.15
.

Note that if
\nu
is a strong solution to (

MF

) automatically
(\nu,\kappa^{+}_{\nu},\kappa_{\nu}^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
.

Vice versa, if
(\nu,\kappa^{+}_{\nu},\kappa_{\nu}^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
then
\nu_{t}
is a strong solution. Namely, any TV-absolutely continuous curve
\nu_{t}
possesses a common dominating measure
\ell\in\Gamma
, which implies
\kappa_{\nu_{t}}^{\pm}\ll\ell+\gamma
. By Lemma
2.11
the curve
\nu
is indeed a a.e. differentiable mapping to
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV})

Lemma 2.16
.

For any
\bar{\nu}\in\Gamma
there exist a unique strong solution
\nu_{t}
to (

MF

) such that
\nu_{0}=\bar{\nu}
.

Moreover, if
\bar{\nu}\ll\gamma
, then also
\nu_{t}\ll\gamma
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

The proof is an adaptation from
[
FM04
, Proposition 7.2]
, which is stated for Lebesgue absolutely continuous measures over
\mathcal{T}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In short, the linear dependence of the birth flux on the mass of
\nu
gives a bound on this mass uniform in time, in which case both
\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}
are Lipschitz in
\nu
on
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|)
, and classical existence theory can be applied.

Proof.

First, note that for the linear case of

\partial_{t}\nu_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)=b_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)-c_{t}(x)\nu_{t}(\mathrm{d}x),

with
c_{t}\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
uniformly bounded and
b_{s}\in\Gamma
with
\int_{0}^{T}\|b_{s}\|_{TV}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
with a common dominating measure, it is easy to verify that a unique strong non-negative solution exists and is given by

\nu_{t}:=e^{-\int_{0}^{t}c_{s}(x)\mathrm{d}s}\left(\int_{0}^{t}b_{s}e^{\int_{0}^{s}c_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r}\mathrm{d}s+\nu_{0}\right).

We now set
\nu^{0}_{t}:=\bar{\nu}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and perform the implicit Picard iteration

\partial_{t}\nu^{k+1}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)=\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu^{k})\rangle\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)-\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu^{k}\rangle\nu^{k+1}(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad\nu^{k+1}_{0}:=\bar{\nu},

i.e.
\nu^{k+1}=(\mathcal{G}\nu^{k})
with

(\mathcal{G}\nu)_{t}(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu_{r}\rangle\mathrm{d}s}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu_{s})\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\rangle e^{\int_{0}^{s}\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu_{r}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}r}\mathrm{d}s+\bar{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)\right).

It is straightforward to check that for all
t\in[0,T]

\sup_{k\geq 1}\nu^{k}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\leq e^{\|c\|_{\infty}\gamma(\mathcal{T})t}\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq e^{\|c\|_{\infty}\gamma(\mathcal{T})T}\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})=:C.

We will show that
\mathcal{G}
is contractive under a suitable metric on the space of curves with initial data
\bar{\nu}
and mass bounded by
C
. This implies there exists a
TV
-absolutely continuous curve
\nu
such that

\nu_{t}-\nu_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\left(\kappa_{\nu_{r}}+\kappa_{\nu_{r}}^{-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mbox{for all $s,t$ with $0\leq s,t\leq T$.}

Moreover, since in the iterations
\nu^{k}\ll\bar{\nu}+\gamma
for all
\nu
it is clear that we obtain strong solutions in
L^{1}(\bar{\nu}+\gamma)
. In particular, for
\bar{\nu}\ll\gamma
we have
\nu_{t}\ll\gamma
for all
t\in[0,T]
as well.

Now, note that
\langle c(x,\cdot),\nu\rangle
depends Lipschitz on
\nu
in
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV})
due to the uniform bound on mass. This implies that there exists a constant
K
such that for any two admissible curves
\nu,\tilde{\nu}
:

\|(\mathcal{G}\nu)_{t}-(\mathcal{G}\tilde{\nu})_{t}\|_{TV}\leq K\int_{0}^{t}\|\nu_{s}-\tilde{\nu}_{s}\|_{TV}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\mbox{for all $t\in[0,T]$.}

Hence, by a Gronwall-type argument, we find that for any
\varepsilon>0
for all
t\in[0,T]

\|(\mathcal{G}\nu)_{t}-\mathcal{G}(\tilde{\nu})_{t}\|_{TV}e^{-(K+\varepsilon)t}\leq\frac{K}{K+\varepsilon}\left(\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|\nu_{s}-\tilde{\nu}_{s}\|_{TV}e^{-(K+\varepsilon)s}\right),

thus yielding the contraction required to apply the Banach fixed-point theorem.
∎

Finally, for the use in entropic propagation chaos of Theorem
5.4
, it is convenient to characterize the conditions for which
u_{t}
is bounded from above and below. The following statement follows directly from a Gronwall-type argument.

Lemma 2.17
.

Suppose
\nu_{0}
is such that
C^{-1}\leq\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}/\mathrm{d}\gamma(x)<C
for some constant
C>0
and all
x\in\mathcal{T}
. Then there exist a constant
C_{T}>0
such that for the corresponding solution

C_{T}^{-1}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}(x)<C_{T},\qquad\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathcal{T},\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T].

2.3.
Variational characterization

We will now prove the non-negativity of our EDP-functional
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
and the characterization of strong solutions to (

MF

) as minimizers of
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
. To do so we first need the prove the chain rule for the free energy
\mathcal{F}_{MF}
along curves with finite
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
.

There is an important technical issue concerning the Fisher information, in the sense that on curves with finite
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
the chain rule inequality holds for the following replacement:

\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\log u\right)\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}=\int_{u>0}c_{\nu}(x)\left(\sqrt{u}-1\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma,

for any
\nu\ll\gamma
with
u:=\mathrm{d}\nu/\mathrm{d}\gamma
. Note that
0\leq\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu)\leq\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)
and
\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}=\mathcal{R}_{MF}^{*}(\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{F}_{MF})
.

We will see the same principle arise in Section
3
for the variational characterization of the forward Kolmogorov equation, which is also observed in
[
PRST22
, Section 5]
.

Lemma 2.18
.

For any curve
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
and
\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})<\infty
it holds that
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{t})
is absolutely continuous and a.e. differentiable with

\frac{\mathrm{d}\,}{\mathrm{d}\,t}\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t},\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

Moreover, for such a curve

\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\geq\mathcal{I}_{MF}^{-}:=\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.

Remark 2.19
.

In fact, for such curves, for a.e.
t
both the terms

\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\pm}_{t},

will be finite, and hence

\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}^{+}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}^{-},

Proof.

Fix any curve
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
. We will show that whenever
\mathcal{I}_{MF}<\infty
the mapping
t\mapsto\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma)
is absolutely continuous and satisfies the chain rule, i.e.

\frac{\mathrm{d}\,\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma)}{\mathrm{d}\,t}=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\mathrm{d}(\lambda^{+}_{t}-\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}_{MF}<\infty
. Since
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}
is bounded from below,
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma)<\infty
implies that

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\qquad\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

In particular for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
it holds that
\nu_{t}\ll\gamma
,
\lambda_{t}^{\pm}\ll\theta_{\nu_{t}}
, and in turn
\theta_{\nu_{t}}\ll\gamma
. In fact, due to TV-continuity of
\nu_{t}
, we have
\nu_{t}\ll\gamma
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover,
\int_{0}^{T}\lambda^{\pm}_{t}(\mathcal{T})<\infty
and
\sup_{t}\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})<\infty
.

Setting
u_{t}:=\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}/\mathrm{d}\gamma
, we have

\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu_{t}}=c_{\nu_{t}}\sqrt{u_{t}}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma,

and in particular
\theta_{\nu_{t}}(\{u_{t}=0\})=0
. Similarly,
\lambda_{t}^{\pm}\ll\theta_{\nu_{t}}
for a.e.
t
and hence
u_{t}>0
for
\lambda^{\pm}_{t},\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}
-a.e.
x
for such
t
as well. Furthermore, since for a.e.
t
we have
\lambda_{t}^{\pm}\ll\theta_{\nu_{t}}\ll\gamma
we find by Lemma
2.11
that
u:[0,T]\to L^{1}(\mathcal{T},\gamma)
is absolutely continuous and differentiable at a.e.
r\in[0,T]
.

Consider any such
r
with
\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{r},\lambda_{r}^{+},\lambda_{r}^{-}),\mathcal{D}(\nu_{r})<\infty
. By Lemma
2.9
, for any
w\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})
,

(2.14)

\left|\int_{\mathcal{T}}w\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}\right|\leq\int_{\mathcal{T}}|w|\,\mathrm{d}|\lambda_{r}|\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{r},\lambda^{+}_{r},\lambda^{-}_{r})+\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(w)\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu_{r}}.

Now let
\phi_{m}
be the convex and uniformly Lipschitz regularizations of
\phi
constructed by using the truncations
\phi_{m}^{\prime}:=[\phi^{\prime}]_{m}=\max\{\min\{\phi,m\},-m\}
and
\phi(s):=\int_{1}^{s}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z
. Note that
\phi_{m}^{\prime}
converges pointwise to
\phi^{\prime}
, and both
\phi_{m}
and
|\phi^{\prime}_{m}|
converge monotonically to
\phi
and
|\phi^{\prime}|
respectively.

Moreover, note that
\phi^{\prime}(u_{r})=\log u_{r}
is
\theta_{\nu_{r}}
-a.e. finite, and similarly
\lambda^{\pm}_{r}
-a.e. as well. Therefore, since
\Psi^{*}
is even and monotone on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we derive

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\phi^{\prime}_{m}(u_{r}))\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu_{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\phi^{\prime}(u_{r}))\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu_{r}}=\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{r}).

Recall that
\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{r})\leq\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{r})
. By substituting
w=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\phi_{m}^{\prime}
in (
2.14
) we find

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}|\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u_{r})|\,\mathrm{d}|\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}|\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{r},\lambda^{+}_{r},\lambda^{-}_{r})+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{r}),

and after a monotone convergence argument

(2.15)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi^{\prime}(u_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}|\phi^{\prime}(u_{r})|\,\mathrm{d}|\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}|\leq\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{r},\lambda^{+}_{r},\lambda^{-}_{r})+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{r}).

Note that for every
m
the function
\phi_{m}
is smooth and uniformly Lipschitz, thus the functional
\int\phi_{m}(u_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma
is
\|\cdot\|_{TV}
-Lipschitz continuous and hence absolutely continuous by TV-regularity of
\nu_{r}
. Moreover, since
\lambda_{r}^{\pm}\ll\gamma
and
u_{r}
is a.e. differentiable in
L^{1}(X,\gamma)
it is straightforward to check that

\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{m}(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma-\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{m}(u_{s})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u_{r})\,\mathrm{d}(\lambda^{+}_{r}-\lambda^{-}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mbox{for all $s,t\in[0,T]$}.

Therefore, since
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma)
is finite by assumption and the functionals
\int\phi_{m}(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma
converge monotonically to
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma)
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma-\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi(u_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{m\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{T}}|\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u_{r})|\,\mathrm{d}|\lambda_{r}^{+}-\lambda_{r}^{-}|\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{r},\lambda_{r}^{+},\lambda_{r}^{-})+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{r})\right)\,\mathrm{d}r.

In particular
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma)
is finite for all
t\in[0,T]
, and after repeating the argument for
s,t\in[0,T]
we conclude by a dominated convergence argument that

\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma-\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi(u_{s})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi^{\prime}(u_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r,

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{MF}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi^{\prime}(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}+\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi^{\prime}(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.

∎

We are now finally in a position to prove Theorem
2.7
. With the chain rule above all that remains is on one hand showing that
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda_{t}^{-})=0
implies that
\lambda_{t}^{\pm}=\kappa_{\nu_{t}}^{\pm}
for a.e.
t
, and on the other hand, showing that if
\nu
is a strong solution it holds that
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{MF}(\nu,\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\kappa_{\nu}^{-})=0
and
\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}=\mathcal{D}_{MF}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. The second part again involves proving a chain rule, but now along the solution curve.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

First, consider any
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
, and
\mathcal{I}_{MF}=0
. By Lemma
2.18
,

\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi^{\prime}(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Now, recall that
\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}=c_{\nu}\sqrt{u}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma
. Setting
g_{t}^{\pm}:=\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\pm}_{t}/\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}
, it holds that
\log(u_{t})\,g_{t}^{\pm}<\infty
for
\theta_{\nu_{t}}
-a.e.
x
and a.e.
t
, and by the inequality (
2.15
) that
|\log u_{t}|\,|g_{t}^{+}-g_{t}^{-}|
is
\theta_{\nu_{t}}
-integrable. Therefore, by straightforward algebraic manipulations we find that for a.e.
t
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi^{\prime}(u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}+\mathcal{D}_{MF}^{-}(\nu_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\left(\phi(g^{+}_{t})+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log(u_{t})g^{+}_{t}+\phi^{*}(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log u_{t})+\phi(g^{-}_{t})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log(u_{t})g^{-}_{t}+\phi^{*}(-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log u_{t})\right)\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu_{t}}.

Due to the duality between
\phi
and
\phi^{*}
this expression is zero if only if
\theta_{\nu_{t}}
-a.e.

g_{t}^{\pm}=(\phi^{\prime})^{-1}(\mp\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log u_{t}).

Recalling that
\theta_{\nu}=c_{\nu}\sqrt{u}\gamma
,
\kappa_{\nu}^{+}=c_{\nu}\gamma
and
\kappa^{-}_{\nu}=c_{\nu}u\gamma
we find that indeed for a.e.
t
,

\lambda^{\pm}_{t}=\kappa_{\nu_{t}}^{\pm}.

Vice versa, assume that
\nu_{t}
is a strong solution with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
. Recall that
\nu_{t}\ll\gamma
for all
t\in[0,T]
by Lemma
2.16
, and hence
\kappa_{\nu_{t}}^{\pm}\ll\gamma
as well. Therefore we can again write
u_{t}:=\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}/\mathrm{d}\gamma
,
\kappa_{\nu}^{+}=c_{\nu}\gamma
,
\kappa^{-}_{\nu}=c_{\nu}u\gamma
and
\theta_{\nu}=c_{\nu}\sqrt{u}\gamma
. Moreover,
u:[0,T]\to L^{1}(\mathcal{T},\gamma)
is absolutely continuous and a.e. differentiable, and thus for every regularized entropy function:

\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{m}(u_{T})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma-\int_{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{m}(u_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{T}}c_{\nu_{t}}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u_{t})(1-u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\gamma\,\mathrm{d}t.

Note that the latter expression is non-positive since
\phi^{\prime}(z)(z-1)
is non-negative, due to the convexity of
\phi_{m}
and
\phi_{m}(1)=0
. Moreover, recall that the regularized entropies converge for every
\nu
, are non-negative, and
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma)<0
by assumption. Therefore

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{T}}c_{\nu_{t}}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u_{t})(u_{t}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma)<\infty.

It is clear that to obtain
\mathcal{I}_{MF}=0
it is sufficient to prove that for any
\nu
with
\nu\ll\gamma
,

\frac{1}{2}\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}}c_{\nu}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u)(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=\mathcal{R}(\nu,\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\kappa_{\nu}^{-})+\mathcal{D}(\nu).

By non-negativity of the integrand both

\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{u=0}c_{\nu}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u)(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma<\infty.

and

\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{u>0}c_{\nu}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u)(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma<\infty.

Since
\phi^{\prime}(0)=-m
this implies that in fact for all
m

\int_{u=0}c_{\nu}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u)(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=m\int_{u=0}c_{\nu}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma,

but since the former is finite after taking the limit
m\to\infty
, we deduce that

\int_{u=0}c_{\nu}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=0,

and hence
\gamma(\{u=0,c_{\nu}>0)=0
. Moreover, by monotone convergence we have

\int_{u>0}c_{\nu}\log(u)(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma=\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{u>0}c_{\nu}\phi_{m}^{\prime}(u)(u-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma.

Note by straightforward algebraic manipulation that

\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log(z)(z-1)=\phi(\sqrt{z})\sqrt{z}+\phi(1/\sqrt{z})\sqrt{z}+(\sqrt{z}-1)^{2}\,\qquad\mbox{ for all }z>0.

Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{u>0}c_{\nu}\log(u)(u-1)\mathrm{d}\gamma

\displaystyle=\int_{u>0,\,c_{\nu}>0}c_{\nu}\left(\phi\left(\sqrt{u}\right)\sqrt{u}+\phi\left(1/\sqrt{u}\right)\sqrt{u(x)}+(\sqrt{u}-1)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\gamma

\displaystyle=\int_{u>0,\,c_{\nu}>0}\left(\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}+\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{-}}{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}+c_{\nu}(\sqrt{u}-1)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\gamma.

Since all terms are non-negative we can separate terms and reduce the expression to

\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\int_{u>0}c_{\nu}\log(u)(u-1)\mathrm{d}\gamma=\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\kappa_{\nu}^{-})+\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu).

Here the equality follows from the fact that
\gamma(\{u=0,c_{\nu}>0)=0
and hence

\int_{u>0,\,c_{\nu}>0}c_{\nu}(\sqrt{u}-1)^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{\mathcal{T}}c_{\nu}(\sqrt{u}-1)^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma=\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu),

i.e.
\mathcal{D}_{MF}^{-}(\nu)=\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)
, and

\int_{u>0,\,c_{\nu}>0}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta_{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\mathrm{d}\gamma=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}|\theta_{\nu}).

∎

3.
Forward Kolmogorov equation

In the introduction, we discussed how the BPDL model describes a measure-valued process
\nu^{n}_{t}
in
\Gamma
involving particles being created and annihilated, with the corresponding Forward Kolmogorov equation

(
\sf FKE_{n}
)

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}=Q_{n}^{*}\mathsf{P}_{t},

where
\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
Q_{n}^{*}
is the dual of the infinitesimal generator
Q_{n}
with

(3.1)

\qquad(Q_{n}F)(\nu)=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\big(F(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\big)\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)+n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\big(F(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\big)\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x),

for all
F\in C_{c}(\Gamma)
. Throughout this section the parameter
n>0
will be fixed.

In the case of
\mathcal{T}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
it is shown in
[
FM04
]
that a measure-valued process with generator
Q_{n}
exists, and is in fact a jump process in
\Gamma
corresponding to the jump kernel
\bar{\kappa}_{n}
shown below. However, for our general setting with
\mathcal{T}
a compact Polish space, we will take (

FKE
n

) simply as a starting point, and do not consider the existence or convergence of the measure-valued process
\nu_{t}^{n}
itself—even though we will sometimes borrow the language of jump processes for illustration purposes.

In this section, we will state the general version of Theorem
1.6
, by showing that a detailed balance condition holds, establishing a generalized gradient structure for the Forward-Kolmogorov equation, and characterizing the solutions as minimizers of corresponding EDP-functionals. Similar to Section
2
we first give an overview of the ingredients to state the main results and then leave the proofs for the existence of solutions and the variational characterization to Sections
3.2
and
3.3
.

Note that due to the fact that

\sup_{\nu\in\Gamma}\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})=+\infty,

the operator
Q_{n}
is not bounded on
\mathcal{B}_{b}(\Gamma)
. If it were, suitable solutions and possible variational formulation would fall into the framework of
[
PRST22
]
, where triples
(V,\pi,\kappa)
are considered, with
V
a Polish space,
\pi
a finite measure, and
\kappa(x,\mathrm{d}y)
a jump kernel satisfying a detailed balance condition with respect to
\pi
and the boundedness condition

\sup_{x}\int_{V}k(x,\mathrm{d}y)<\infty.

They construct solutions to the forward Kolmogorov equation that are absolutely continuous to
\pi
and characterize them as minimizers of a suitable EDP functional involving the net flux. In this section, we generalize part of this framework to unbounded kernels and so-called one-way or uni-directional fluxes and tailor it to our setting of interacting particle systems.

Namely, let the rescaled empirical measure mapping
L_{n}:\coprod_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{T}^{N}\to\Gamma
be given as

(3.2)

L_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}.

and let
\Gamma_{n}\subset\Gamma
be the space of finite
positive
discrete measures with common unit weight
\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}
, i.e.

(3.3)

\Gamma_{n}:=L_{n}\left(\coprod_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{T}^{N}\right).

Note that the operators
Q_{n},Q_{n}^{*}
can be represented as

\displaystyle(Q_{n}F)(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{n}}\left(F(\eta)-F(\nu)\right)\bar{\kappa}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta),

\displaystyle(Q_{n}^{*}\mathsf{P})(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\eta\in\Gamma_{n}}\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\eta,\mathrm{d}\nu)-\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\int_{\eta\in\Gamma_{n}}\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta),

where
\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\nu,\cdot)\in\mathcal{M}^{+}(\Gamma_{n})
for all
\nu\in\Gamma_{n}
is a jump kernel over
\Gamma_{n}
given by

(3.4)

\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta):=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)+n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x).

Moreover, we consider Poisson measures
\Pi_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{n})
induced by the reference measure
\gamma
. Namely, with the measure
\pi_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\coprod_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{T}^{N})
given by

(3.5)

\pi_{n}:=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\gamma^{\otimes N},

we define

(3.6)

\Pi_{n}:=(L_{n})_{\#}\pi.

We will show in Lemma
3.12
that the measures
\Pi_{n}
are invariant measures of (

FKE
n

) and that
\bar{\kappa}_{n}
satisfies the detailed balance condition with respect to
\Pi_{n}
, i.e. we have the symmetry

(3.7)

\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)=\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta).

It is straightforward to check that even though
\bar{\kappa}_{n}
