is unbounded, we still have the weighted integrability condition

\sup_{\nu\in\Gamma_{n}}\left\{(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{-2})\int_{\Gamma_{n}}\bar{\kappa}_{\nu}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)\right\}<+\infty.

Therefore we can still bootstrap from gradient-flow solutions in the sense of
[
PRST22
]
for regularized triples
(\Gamma_{n},\Pi_{n},\bar{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}_{n})
, after passing from a net flux to a one-way flux formulation, see Appendix
A
, to obtain unique gradient-flow solutions as defined in Section
3.2
.

To discuss the continuity equation and the dissipation potentials properly, we need to introduce some additional notation. We define the following creation and annihilation operators:

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}^{n,+}

\displaystyle:\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T}\to\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T},\qquad\mathsf{T}^{n,+}(\nu,x)=(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)=:(\mathsf{T}_{x}^{n,+}\nu,x),

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}^{n,-}

\displaystyle:\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T}\to\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T},\qquad\mathsf{T}^{n,-}(\nu,x)=(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)=:(\mathsf{T}_{x}^{n,-}\nu,x),

with the convention that
\mathsf{T}^{n,-}(\nu,x)=\nu
if
x\notin\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
. Note that
\mathsf{T}^{n,-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,+}=\mathsf{Id}
always holds, and
\mathsf{T}^{n,+}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,-}(\nu,x)=(\nu,x)
whenever
x\in\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
.

We further define the discrete
\Gamma_{n}
-gradients
\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}:C_{c}(\Gamma_{n})\to C_{c}(\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T})
:

(3.9)

(\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}F)(\nu,x):=n(F(\mathsf{T}_{x}^{n,\pm}\nu)-F(\nu)),

and the corresponding
\Gamma_{n}
-divergence
\overline{\text{div}}^{n,\pm}:\mathcal{M}_{loc}^{+}(\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma_{n})
, dual to
\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}
, given by

(3.10)

(\overline{\text{div}}^{n,\pm}\mathsf{J})=n\left(\mathsf{p}^{\Gamma_{n}}_{\#}\mathsf{J}-(\mathsf{p}^{\Gamma_{n}}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm})_{\#}\mathsf{J}\right),

where
\mathsf{p}^{\Gamma_{n}}:{\Gamma_{n}}\times\mathcal{T}\to\Gamma_{n}
denotes the projection to the first variable.

We consider the familes of curves satisfying

(
\mathsf{CE}_{n}
)

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}+(\overline{\text{div}}^{n,+}\mathsf{J}_{t}^{+})+(\overline{\text{div}}^{n,-}\mathsf{J}_{t}^{-})=0

in the following appropriate distributional sense.

Definition 3.1

(Continuity equation)
.

A triple
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
satisfies the continuity equation
\mathsf{CE}_{n}
, if

(1)

the curve
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{n})
is narrowly continuous,

(2)

the Borel family
(\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T})
satisfies

\mathrm{supp}(\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\subseteq\left\{(\nu,x)\,:\,\nu(\mathcal{T})\geq\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n},\,x\in\mathrm{supp}(\nu)\right\},

(3)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
,

(4)

for every
s,t\in[0,T]
and all
F\in C_{c}(\Gamma_{n})

(3.11)

\int_{\Gamma_{n}}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma_{n}}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T}}\left((\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+(\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r.

Throughout we will call arbitrary measures
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T})

admissible
if

\mathrm{supp}(\mathsf{J}^{-})\subseteq\left\{(\nu,x)\,:\,\nu(\mathcal{T})\geq\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n},\,x\in\mathrm{supp}(\nu)\right\}

and

\int_{\Gamma_{n}\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}<\infty.

Moreover, since
\Gamma_{n}
is a closed subspace of the Polish space
\Gamma
, the extension of
\mathsf{P}
to
\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
and the extension of
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
to
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
are well-defined. For simplicity we will simply refer to them as
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
as well, and drop the
n
-dependence in most arguments.

It is also clear that for any admissible
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}

(\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}F)(\nu,x):=n\left(F(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\right),\qquad(\nu,x)\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathsf{J}^{\pm})

and in particular (
3.11
) is equivalent to

\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Big(n\big(F(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+n\big(F(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r.

for all
F\in C_{c}(\Gamma)
. Note that this can again be extended to all
F\in\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma)
via a monotone class argument.

Remark 3.2
.

Condition (
2
) represents the restriction that particles can only be deleted if there are at least two particles in the system, consistent with the fact that
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{n})
and hence the underlying process never attains
\nu=0
.

Moreover, condition (
3
) reflects the unboundedness of the observed fluxes
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
, which stems from the unboundedness of the birth/death kernels
\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}
in
\nu
.

Remark 3.3
.

Whenever
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
are of the form

\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\lambda^{\pm}[t,\nu](\mathrm{d}x)

with
\lambda^{\pm}[t,\nu]\in\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
for all
\nu\in\Gamma
and
t\in[0,T]
, the continuity equation (
3.11
) describes the forward Kolmogorov equation corresponding to an interacting birth/death process with the birth/death kernels
\lambda^{\pm}[t,\nu]
depending on both time and the empirical measure of the particles
\nu
. The time-dependent jump kernel is then given by

\bar{\kappa}_{n,t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)=n\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\lambda^{+}[t,\nu](\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\lambda^{-}[t,\nu](\mathrm{d}x)\right).

In order to define the dissipation potentials, let us introduce the measures
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}^{+}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})

(3.12)

\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{d}x):=\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x).

Note that for any curve
(\mathsf{P}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
the measures
\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}:=\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}
satisfy the conditions (
2
) and (
3
), where the latter holds because
c(x,x)=0
.

Moreover, as will be shown in Lemma
3.12
, we have the following symmetry

(3.13)

\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}=\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta^{\mp}_{\Pi_{n}}.

from which the detailed balance condition
(
3.7
)
directly follows.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
be the geometric average of
\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta^{\mp}_{\mathsf{P}}
, i.e.

(3.14)

\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x):=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta^{\mp}_{\mathsf{P}})}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

for any dominating measure
\Sigma
.

The dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}_{n}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\times\mathcal{M}_{loc}^{+}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
and dual dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\times\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})^{2}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\Theta^{n,+}_{\mathsf{P}})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{-}|\Theta^{n,-}_{\mathsf{P}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{n}^{*}(\mathsf{P},\omega^{+},\omega^{-})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(e^{\omega^{+}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta^{n,+}_{\mathsf{P}}+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(e^{\omega^{-}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta^{n,-}_{\mathsf{P}}

For the free energy
\mathcal{F}_{n}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\to[0,+\infty]
and Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{n}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\to[0,+\infty]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle:=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &H^{2}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+},\mathsf{T}_{\#}^{n,-}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})+H^{2}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-},\mathsf{T}_{\#}^{n,+}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+})\qquad&&\mbox{if $\mathsf{P}\ll\Pi_{n}$,}\\
&+\infty&&\mbox{otherwise}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

For the
EDP-functional

\mathcal{I}_{n}:\mathsf{CE}_{n}\to[0,+\infty]
for all curves with
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty

\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}_{t}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{n}(\nu_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{n}(\nu_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Remark 3.5
.

The definition of
\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,\pm}
is independent of the dominating measure
\Sigma
. Moreover, formally

\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}(\nu,x)=\sqrt{(\mathsf{P}(\nu)\kappa^{+}[\nu])(\mathsf{P}(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})\kappa^{-}[\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}]),

i.e. it represents the geometric mean of the expected fluxes going forwards and backwards along the transition
\nu\leftrightarrow\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}
.

In addition, due to the symmetry (
3.13
) the measures
\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,\pm}
simplify whenever
\mathsf{P}\ll\Pi_{n}
, i.e. if
\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}=U\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}
we have

\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\sqrt{U(\nu)U(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})}\,\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x).

Remark 3.6
.

Note that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a jointly convex function in
(\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{T}_{\#}^{n,\mp}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mp})
, and lower semicontinuous if
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is bounded.
Moreover, it is straightforward to check that whenever
\mathsf{P}\ll\Pi_{n}
with
\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}=U\Pi_{n}
it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\left(\sqrt{U(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})}-\sqrt{U(\nu)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{+}_{\Pi_{n}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\left(\sqrt{U(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})}-\sqrt{U(\nu)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{-}_{\Pi_{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\left(\sqrt{U(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})}-\sqrt{U(\nu)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\Pi_{n}}.

Finally, for technical purposes, we also introduce a version for net fluxes.

Definition 3.7
.

The
upward
net flux
\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}
is defined as

\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}:=\mathsf{J}^{+}-\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\mathsf{J}^{-}

Note that
\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}(\nu,x)
can be interpreted as the net flux along the jump
\nu\leftrightarrow\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}
.

The continuity equation for the net flux reduces to

\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}n\big(F(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r

We are now in a position to give the general version of Theorem
1.6
.

Theorem 3.8
.

For any
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
with
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
we have
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\geq 0
,

(3.15)

\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=0\implies\left\{\begin{aligned} \quad&\mbox{$\mathsf{P}_{t}$ is a weak solution to \eqref{eq:FKEn}}\quad\\
\quad&\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}=\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}\quad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$},\quad\end{aligned}\right.

and there exist a unique gradient-flow solution, i.e. a curve
(\mathsf{P})
such that
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{-})=0
.

Moreover, whenever
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})<\infty
, the chain rule for
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and the net flux holds holds:
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})
is absolutely continuous and

\frac{\mathrm{d}\,}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})=\frac{n}{2}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\log U(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-\log U(\nu))\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$.}

The proof of Theorem
3.8
is postponed to Section
3.3
, and follows from the existence of a gradient-flow solution via EDP-convergence of a sequence of regularized problems established in Section
3.2
, and its uniqueness via a convexity argument.

Remark 3.9
.

Similar to the mean-field case, the non-negativity of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
and the identification of solutions to (

MF

) as null-minimizers of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is related to the formal equivalence

\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is the so-called
Lagrangian
given by

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\mathsf{P}\kappa_{\nu}^{+})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{-}|\mathsf{P}\kappa_{\nu}^{-}).

We discuss the implication of this relation in Appendix
A
.

Remark 3.10

(Net flux)
.

To show the existence of gradient-flow solutions in the sense of null-minimizers of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
we will have to jump from gradient-flow solutions in the sense of
[
PRST22
]
, see Theorem
3.20
. The expressions for net-fluxes are in fact contractions of those for one-way or uni-directional fluxes, as discussed in Section
A
, which we use to show that the two notions of gradient-flow solutions are equivalent.

3.1.
A priori estimates

Below we will state the estimates and identities necessary to prove the chain rule and establish the existence of solutions.

Recall that
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}
satisfies the same restrictions (Conditions (
2
) and (
3
)) as the fluxes
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
. This is easily verified, but since we will use it repeatedly let us state it here precisely.

Lemma 3.11
.

For any
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{n})

\mathrm{supp}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})\subseteq\left\{(\nu,x)\,:\,\nu(\mathcal{T})\geq\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n},\,x\in\mathrm{supp}(\nu)\right\}.

In particular, for any
\omega\in C_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega(\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm},

and

\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}=\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}.

Finally,

\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}=\Theta^{n,\mp}_{\mathsf{P}}.

The above identities allow us to prove the symmetry condition that implies the detailed balance condition (
3.7
).

Lemma 3.12

(Detailed balance)
.

\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}=\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta^{\mp}_{\Pi_{n}}.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
\omega\in C_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
, and for any ordered collection of
N
variables in
\mathcal{T}
set
\mathbf{x}^{N}:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in X^{N}
. We then have the following.

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega(\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{+}_{\Pi_{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N}),x\right)\kappa^{+}\left[L_{n}(\mathbf{x^{N}})\right](\mathrm{d}x)\right)\gamma^{\otimes N}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N}),

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega(\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta^{-}_{\Pi_{n}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x\right)\kappa^{-}\left[L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N})\right](\mathrm{d}x)\right)\gamma^{\otimes N}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N}).

Since
\kappa^{-}[\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{y}]=0
for any
y\in\mathcal{T}
, the sum in the right-hand side of the last expression starts from
N=2
, thus reducing the expression to

\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N+1}}{(N+1)!}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N+1}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}\omega\left(L(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x\right)\kappa^{-}\left[L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})\right](\mathrm{d}x)\right)\gamma^{\otimes(N+1)}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N+1}),

It is clear that, for our desired equality, it is enough to show that for every
N
,

\displaystyle n\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N+1}}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x\right)c(x,y)\,L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)\right)\,\gamma^{\otimes(N+1)}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N+1})

\displaystyle=(N+1)\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\left(\int_{X^{2}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N}),x\right)c(x,y)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N})(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\,\gamma^{\otimes N}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N}).

To do so, note that since
c(x,x)=0
,

\displaystyle n\int_{X^{2}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x\right)c(x,y)\,L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N+1}\sum_{j\neq i}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x_{i}},x_{i}\right)c(x_{i},x_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt=\sum_{i=1}^{N+1}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x_{i}},x_{i}\right)c(x_{i},y)\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x_{i}}\right)(\mathrm{d}y).

Hence, by symmetry of
\gamma^{\otimes(N+1)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N+1}}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N+1}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x_{i}},x_{i}\right)c(x_{i},y)\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N+1})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x_{i}}\right)(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\gamma^{\otimes(N+1)}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N+1})

\displaystyle=(N+1)\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}\omega\left(L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N}),x\right)c(x,y)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)L_{n}(\mathbf{x}^{N})(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\gamma^{\otimes N}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{N}),

as desired.
∎

Recall from Lemma
2.9
that that

\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2}),

where
M:=(1+\gamma(\mathcal{T}))\|c\|_{\infty}
. Now let

M_{n}:=\max\bigl\{1+2/n^{2},2\bigr\}M,

and the jointly convex and lower semicontinuous function
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}\to[0,+\infty]
given by

\Upsilon(w,u,v):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{uv}&\quad\mbox{if $w=0$,}\\
\phi\left(\frac{w}{\sqrt{uv}}\right)\sqrt{uv}&\quad\mbox{if $u,v>0$,}\\
+\infty&\quad\mbox{if $w>0$, and either $u=0$ or $v=0$.}\end{cases}

We then have the following result.

Lemma 3.13
.

The following statements hold:

(i)

For all
\mathsf{P}

\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{-2}\,\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{d}y)\leq\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{d}y)\leq M_{n}.

(ii)

For any
\mathsf{P}
, admissible
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
, and net flux
\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}=\mathsf{J}^{+}-\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\mathsf{J}^{-}
,
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
, we have

\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}|\omega|\,\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}|\,\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}.

Moreover,

(3.16a)

\displaystyle\phi\left(1\vee\frac{1}{M_{n}}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(X)^{2})^{-1}\,\mathsf{J}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\right)M_{n}\,\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}),

(3.16b)

\displaystyle\Psi\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(X))^{-1}\,|\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}|(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\right)M_{n}\,\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}).

(iii)

For all admissible
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
,

(3.17)

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{\pm}|\Theta^{n,\pm})=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mp})}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\right)\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

for any common dominating measure
\Sigma
. Moreover, if
\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}=U\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}
,

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{\pm}|\Theta^{n,\pm})=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}},U(\nu),U(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}.

Remark 3.14
.

Since
M_{n}\leq 3M
for all
n\geq 1
the estimates (
3.16
) are uniform in
n
, which we will use in the EDP-convergence to establish tightness of sequences
\mathsf{J}^{n,\pm}
under bound on
\mathcal{I}_{n}
. Moreover, the representation (
3.17
) is used to deduce the lower-semicontinuity of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
for sequences of curves.

Proof.

(i)
For any
x^{*}\in\mathcal{T}
,
\nu\in\Gamma
, we have

\displaystyle\max\{\kappa^{\pm}(\mathcal{T}),\kappa^{\pm}[\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{x^{*}}(\nu)](\mathcal{T})\}

\displaystyle\leq M\max\left\{1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2},\,1+(\mathsf{T}^{n,+}_{x^{*}}(\nu))(\mathcal{T})^{2},\,1+(\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{x^{*}}(\nu))(\mathcal{T})^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq M_{n}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})

due to the inequality

1+(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}+z)^{2}\leq 1+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n^2}+2z^{2},\qquad\mbox{ for all }z\geq 0.

In particular,

\max\left\{\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm},\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}\right\}\leq M_{n},

and hence the desired statement follows after applying Jensen’s inequality.

(ii)
By duality we have for any
\omega\in\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega^{+}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{+}+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{-}\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(e^{\omega^{+}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(e^{\omega^{-}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,-}.

Substituting
\omega^{+}=\omega
,
\omega^{-}=-\omega\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,-}
and using the fact that
\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\Theta^{n,-}_{\mathsf{P}}=\Theta^{n,+}_{\mathsf{P}}
we derive

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\omega\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}.

Since
\Psi^{*}
is even we can replace
\omega
and
\mathsf{J}
by their absolutes in the inequality, after substituting for
\omega
appropriately, and we conclude with a monotone convergence argument. The inequalities (
3.16a
) and (
3.16b
) now follow similarly as in Lemma
2.9
via respectively Jensen’s inequality and a dual approach.

(iii)
Let us only consider
\mathsf{J}^{+}
,
\Theta^{n,+}
(the case for
\mathsf{J}^{-}
,
\Theta^{n,-}
is similar). Suppose
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\Theta^{n,+})<\infty
and recall that

\Theta^{n,+}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x):=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{+}_{\mathsf{P}}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta^{-}_{\mathsf{P}})}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

where
\Sigma
is a dominating measure, e.g
\Sigma=\vartheta^{+}_{\mathsf{P}}+\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta^{-}_{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
\mathsf{J}^{+}\ll\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}\ll\Sigma
, and it follows that
\mathsf{J}^{+}
-a.e.
\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}/\mathrm{d}\Sigma
,
\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})/\mathrm{d}\Sigma>0
, from which one can easily verifies (
3.17
).

Vice versa, suppose that

\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\right)\mathrm{d}\Sigma<\infty,

for some dominating measure
\Sigma
. Then again
\mathsf{J}^{+}
-a.e. we have that
\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}/\mathrm{d}\Sigma
,
\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})/\mathrm{d}\Sigma>0
, and by super-linearity of
\phi
deduce that in fact
\mathsf{J}^{+}\ll\tilde{\Sigma}
for any dominating measure of
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}
and
\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-}
, which together implies
\mathsf{J}^{+}\ll\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}
and the result follows similarly as above.
∎

Finally, we discuss the time-regularity of
\mathsf{P}_{t}
for admissible curves and state the analog of Lemma
2.11
. Let the weighted total variation metric
d_{TV,w}
be given as

(3.18)

\displaystyle d_{TV,w}(\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{P}^{1}-\mathsf{P}^{2}|.

Note that
d_{TV,w}
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the narrow topology, and while convergence in
d_{TV,w}
does not directly imply narrow convergence, it does so on narrowly pre-compact sets.

Lemma 3.15
.

For any
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
we have
\mbox{ for all }s,t\in[0,T]
:

(3.19)

d_{TV,w}(\mathsf{P}_{s},\mathsf{P}_{t})\leq 4n\max\Bigl\{1+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n^2},2\Bigr\}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+\mathsf{J}^{-}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Suppose in addition that
\mathsf{P}_{t}\ll\Pi_{n},\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}\ll\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and set

\displaystyle\ell:=(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\Pi_{n},\qquad\Sigma^{\pm}:=(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\Pi_{n}}.

Then there exists an absolutely continuous and a.e. differentiable map
U:[a,b]\to L^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma),\ell)
and maps
G^{\pm}:[0,T]\to L^{1}(\Sigma^{\pm})
such that
U_{t}=\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}/\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}
,
G_{t}^{\pm}=\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}/\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}
, and

(3.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U_{t}(\nu)

\displaystyle=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}(G_{t}^{-}(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)-G_{t}^{+}(\nu,x))\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle+n\int_{\mathcal{T}}(G_{t}^{+}(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)-G_{t}^{-}(\nu,x))\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x).

Alternatively, in terms of the net-flux
\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}=G\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}
with
G^{\mathrm{net}}:=G^{+}-G^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,+}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U_{t}(\nu)=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)-n\int_{\mathcal{T}}G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}(\nu,x)\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x).

Remark 3.16
.

Note that the estimate (
3.19
) for the weighted total variation metric blows up as
n\to\infty
. For the proof of EDP-convergence we instead use a weaker metric, the transportation-like metric
W
defined by (
4.4
), which does behave uniform-in-
n
for a sequence of curves with finite
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}
.

Proof.

Due to the continuity equation and after a monotone class argument, we have the crude estimate

\left|\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}_{t}-\mathsf{P}_{s})\right|\leq n\int_{s}^{t}\left[\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(|F(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})|+|F(\nu)|)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(|F(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})|+|F(\nu)|)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-}\right]\mathrm{d}r,

for any
F\in\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma)
.
Now fix
F\in\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma)
, and let
K:=\sup_{\nu\in\Gamma}F(\nu)(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})
. Note that by the bounds of Lemma
3.13
for any
\nu\in\Gamma_{n}
, we have the following estimates

\displaystyle|F|(\nu)

\displaystyle\leq K(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle|F|(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})

\displaystyle\leq K\big(1+(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{2}\big)^{-1}\leq K(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1},

\displaystyle|F|(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})

\displaystyle\leq K\big(1+(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{-1}{n}+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{2}\big)^{-1}\leq K\max\{1+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n^2},2\}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1},

and therefore

\left|\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}_{t}-\mathsf{P}_{s})\right|\leq 4nK\max\Bigl\{1+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n^2},2\Bigr\}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}^{+}_{r}+\mathsf{J}^{-}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Taking the supremum over all
F\in\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma)
with
\sup_{\nu\in\Gamma}F(\nu)(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})\leq 1
we conclude that

\displaystyle d_{TV,w}(\mathsf{P}_{s},\mathsf{P}_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{P}_{t}^{1}-\mathsf{P}_{s}^{2}|

\displaystyle\leq 4n\max\Bigl\{1+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n^2},2\Bigr\}\int_{s}^{t}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}(\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{+}_{r}+\mathsf{J}^{-}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Next, suppose that
\mathsf{P}_{t}\ll\Pi_{n},\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}\ll\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Let
U_{t}=\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}/\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}
,
G_{t}^{\pm}=\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}/\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}
. Note that by the absolutely continuity of
\mathsf{P}_{t}
with respect to
d_{TV,w}
, the map
t\mapsto U_{t}
is absolutely continuous in
L^{1}(\ell)
. Moreover, for every
F\in\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma)
the continuity equation reads as

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}F(U_{t}-U_{s})\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(F(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu))G_{r}^{+}(\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(F(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu))G_{r}^{-}(\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}r.

But due to Lemma
3.12
, the integrands can be rewritten as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}F(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})G_{r}^{\pm}(\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}F(\nu)G_{r}^{\pm}(\nu\mp\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}F(\nu)G_{r}^{\pm}(\nu\mp\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\mp},

and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}F(U_{t}-U_{s})\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}F(\nu)(G_{r}^{-}(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)-G_{r}^{+}(\nu,x))\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}F(\nu)(G_{r}^{+}(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)-G_{r}^{-}(\nu,x))\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}r,

which is the weak formulation of (
3.20
). Putting in the pre-factors
(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}
to state the expression in terms of the finite measures
\ell
and
\Sigma
, and noting that due to time-regularity
(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\mathsf{P}_{t}
is TV-regular, we can proceed as in Corollary 4.14 of
[
PRST22
]
and conclude the proof after redefining
U,G^{\pm}
on negligible sets.
∎

3.2.
Weak solutions

In this section we will discuss the existence of weak solutions to (

FKE
n

), i.e. solutions to

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}=\overline{\text{div}}^{n,+}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}+\overline{\text{div}}^{n,-}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-},

in appropriate weak form, but with the property that
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})\leq 0
. In the next section we will show that
\mathcal{I}_{n}\geq 0
and that gradient-flow solutions, i.e. those with
\mathcal{I}_{n}=0
, are in fact unique.

Definition 3.17
.

A curve
(\mathsf{P}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a weak solution to (

FKE
n

) if
\mathrm{supp}\,\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\Gamma_{n}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathsf{P}_{t}
is continuous in the narrow topology and for all
s,t\in[0,T]
, and all
F\in C_{c}(\Gamma)
,

\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\left((\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{+}+(\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F)\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r.

Remark 3.18
.

Recall that
\int(1+\nu(X)^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}\leq M_{n}
independently of
\mathsf{P}_{t}
. Hence it is easy to check that
(\mathsf{P})
is a weak solution if and only if
(\mathsf{P},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
.

Moreover, solutions turn out to inherit polynomial mass-estimates from the initial condition, see e.g. Theorem 3.1 of
[
FM04
]
for the case in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. While throughout we do not assume more from the initial condition than having finite entropy with respect to
\Pi_{n}
(which does imply the finiteness of the first moment) and unfortunately arbitrary curves
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
with finite
\mathcal{I}_{n}
do not preserve moment estimates, we will include the statement for completeness.

Lemma 3.19
.

Fix any
p\geq 0
, and assume that
(\mathsf{P})
is a weak solution with initial datum satisfying

\int_{\Gamma}\nu(X)^{p}\,\mathsf{P}_{0}(\mathrm{d}\nu)<\infty.

Then

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\Gamma}\nu(X)^{p}\,\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)<\infty.

Proof.

Set
F(\nu):=f(\nu(X))
with
f(z):=z^{p}
and let
F_{k}(\nu):=f_{k}(\nu(X))
be its sequence of truncations. Then for every
k
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}F_{k}\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}_{t}-\mathsf{P}_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{r}}^{+}+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{r}}^{-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\big(f_{k}(\nu(X)+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n})-f_{k}(\nu(X))\big)\,\mathsf{P}_{r}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}r,

since
f_{k}
is non-decreasing and hence
\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F_{k}\leq 0
. Moreover, note that
z(f(z+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n})-f(z))\leq C_{p,n}\,f(z)
for a suitable constant
C_{p,n}
, and by monotonicity and non-negativeness of
f
the same inequality holds for the truncations
f_{k}
. By a Gronwall-type argument we then obtain

\int_{\Gamma}F_{k}(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}\leq e^{tK_{p,n}}\int_{\Gamma}F_{k}(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{0}\leq e^{tK}\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{0},

with the constant
K_{p,n}=C_{p,n}\|c\|_{\infty}\gamma(X)
independent of
k
. Taking
k\to\infty
we derive the desired inequality by monotone convergence.
∎

We can now state the existence result of a weak solution satisfying one-half of the Energy-Dissipation principle, which is complemented by the chain rule proved in Section
3.3
. The existence proof is one of EDP-convergence (see also Section
5
), bootstrapping from problems with bounded kernels and the results of
[
PRST22
]
.

Theorem 3.20
.

Suppose that

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}|\Pi_{n})<\infty.

Then there exist a weak solution
(\mathsf{P})
with initial datum
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
such that

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 0.

Proof.

Fix any
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
with
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}|\Pi_{n})<\infty
. We proceed by approximating the unbounded kernel
\bar{\kappa}_{n}
with bounded ones. For every
\varepsilon>0
, we introduce the regularized jump kernel
\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)
over
\Gamma
defined by

\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta):=\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon\nu(\mathcal{T})\eta(\mathcal{T})}\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta).

In terms of birth/death kernels this can be rewritten as

\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\kappa^{+,\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)+n\int_{\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\kappa^{-,\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x),

where

\kappa^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\nu}:=\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon\nu(\mathcal{T})(\nu(\mathcal{T})\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n})}\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}.

Note that

(3.21)

\sup_{\nu\in\Gamma}\kappa^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\nu}<\infty\qquad\mbox{ for all }\varepsilon>0.

Correspondingly, we denote
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm,\varepsilon}
,
\Theta^{n,\pm,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}}
,
Q_{n,\varepsilon}
,
Q_{n,\varepsilon}^{*}
,
\mathcal{R}_{n,\varepsilon}
,
\mathcal{D}_{n,\varepsilon}
,
\mathcal{I}_{n,\varepsilon}
,
(\mathsf{FKE}_{n,\varepsilon})
as the relevant quantities, operators, functionals and forward Kolmogorov equations induced by
\kappa^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\nu}
. We will first show existence of gradient-flow solutions for the regularized problems, i.e. curves such that
\mathcal{I}_{n,\varepsilon}=0
, and then construct an appropriate limit curve as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Thus, fix any
\varepsilon>0
. Due to the bound (
3.21
) it is clear that
Q_{n,\varepsilon}
is a bounded operator since

\sup_{\nu\in\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)<\infty.

Moreover, since the prefactor
\nu(\mathcal{T})\eta(\mathcal{T})
is symmetric under swapping of
\nu
and
\eta
, it straightforward to verify that
\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
is still reversible with respect to the same invariant measure
\Pi_{n}
, i.e. we have

\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)=\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\eta,\mathrm{d}\nu).

The triple
(\Gamma,\Pi_{n},\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon})
therefore satisfies the assumptions of
[
PRST22
]
. Keeping in mind the difference in definitions of
\Psi^{*}
due to extra the factor
2
, by
[
PRST22
, Theoren 6.6]
there exist a unique curve
U^{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}([0,T],L^{1}(\Gamma,\Pi_{n}))
such that
U_{0}=\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathsf{P}}/\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}
, and

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}U_{t}(\nu)&=\int_{\Gamma}(U_{t}(\eta)-U_{t}(\nu))\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta),\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$},\\
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{0}|\Pi_{n})-\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{T}|\Pi_{n})&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma\times\Gamma}\Psi\left(U_{t}(\eta)-U_{t}(\nu)\right)\sqrt{U_{t}(\nu)U_{t}(\eta)}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\int_{\Gamma\times\Gamma}\left(\sqrt{U_{t}(\eta)}-\sqrt{U_{t}(\nu)}\right)^{2}\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}\right.

with
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=U_{t}\Pi_{n}
as usual. In particular the entropy
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi_{n})
decreases along the solution and hence

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}|\Pi_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}|\Pi_{n}).

By evenness of
\Psi
, symmetry of
\Pi_{n}\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
and the identity (
A.2
), we can express for any
U
after substituting for
\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\times\Gamma}

\displaystyle\Psi\left(U(\eta)-U(\nu)\right)\sqrt{U(\nu)U(\eta)}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{U(\eta)>0,U(\nu)>0}\phi\left(\sqrt{U(\nu)/U(\eta)}\right)\sqrt{U(\eta)U(\nu)}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{U(\nu+n^{-1}\delta_{x})>0,U(\nu)>0}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+,\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\Theta^{+,n,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}}}\right)\sqrt{U(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})U(\nu)}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\kappa^{+,\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{U(\nu-n^{-1}\delta_{x})>0,U(\nu)>0}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-,\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\Theta^{-,n,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}}}\right)\sqrt{U(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})U(\nu)}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\kappa^{-,\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{n,\varepsilon}\left(\mathsf{P},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-,\varepsilon}\right).

Moreover, it is straightforward to check that

\int_{\Gamma\times\Gamma}\left(\sqrt{U_{t}(\eta)}-\sqrt{U_{t}(\nu)}\right)^{2}\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\bar{\kappa}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)=\mathcal{D}_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathsf{P}),

and therefore with
J^{\pm}:=\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm,\varepsilon}
we conclude

\mathcal{I}_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=0.

Finally, note that by Lemma
3.13
and Remark
3.6

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}\left(\mathsf{J}^{\pm}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+,\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm,\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm,\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mp,\varepsilon})}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\right)\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=2H^{2}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm,\varepsilon},\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mp,\varepsilon}),

for any dominating measure
\Sigma
, which are both non-negative, convex and vaguely lower-semicontinuous functionals of
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm,\varepsilon},\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mp,\varepsilon}
in
\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
, see
[
But89
, Theorem 3.4.3]
.

Next, we consider the sequence of pairs
(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon},\mathsf{J}^{\pm,\varepsilon})
stemming from the regularized problems above, satisfying

\mathcal{I}_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon},\mathsf{J}^{+,\varepsilon},\mathsf{J}^{-,\varepsilon})=0\qquad\mbox{ for all }\varepsilon>0.

As for a priori estimates, we have

(3.22)

\sup_{\varepsilon,t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\Pi_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}|\Pi_{n}),

and

\kappa^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\leq\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}(\mathcal{T})\qquad\mbox{ for all }\varepsilon>0.

From the latter, it can be shown similarly as in Lemma
3.15
that we have the equicontinuity result

d_{TV,w}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{s})\leq 2n\max\{1+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{2}{n^2},2\}|t-s|.

Here
d_{TV,w}
is the weighted total variation-metric defined in (
3.18
) as

\displaystyle d_{TV,w}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{P}^{1}-\mathsf{P}^{2}|,\mbox{ for all }\varepsilon>0,\,\mbox{ for all }s,t\in[0,T].

Recall that
d
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the narrow topology and convergence in
d
implies narrow convergence on narrowly pre-compact sets. Since
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\Pi_{n})
is bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
and
t
and
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\cdot|\Pi_{n})
is narrowly coercive we obtain by a standard Arzelá-Ascoli argument, up to choosing a subsequence, the existence of a curve
t\mapsto\mathsf{P}_{t}
such that

\mathsf{P}_{t}^{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{P}_{t}\quad\mbox{narrowly\; for all $t\in[0,T]$}.

Note that by the estimate (
3.22
) and lower-semicontinuity of the entropy, we have that for every
t\in[0,T]
, the sequence of measures
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{\varepsilon}
converge
setwise
to
\mathsf{P}_{t}
and
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}|\Pi_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}|\Pi_{n})<\infty
. Moreover,
\kappa^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\nu}\nearrow\kappa^{\pm}_{\nu}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for every
\nu
, and hence setwise convergence of
\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
implies setwise convergence on pre-compact sets of
\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}
for

\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\pm,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\mathsf{P}_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\kappa^{\pm,\varepsilon}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x),

see e.g.
[
PRST22
, Lemma 2.4]
for the case of set-wise convergence for bounded jump kernels. In particular we have the vague convergence

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}^{\varepsilon}}\to\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm},\qquad\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}^{\varepsilon}}\to\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}.

It is straightforward to check that we can pass to the limit in the continuity equation (
3.11
), and in particular, derive that
\mathsf{P}
is a weak solution to the unregularized problem.

Finally, recall that
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{T})
is convex in and narrowly lower semicontinuous in
\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{T}
, and as shown above the action
\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}_{n}
is jointly convex and lower semicontinuous in
(\vartheta^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}},\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta^{\mp,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}})
. Proceeding as in Remark
3.6
, we also find that the Fisher information is jointly convex and lower semicontinuous in
(\vartheta^{\pm,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}},\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta^{\mp,\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}})
if
\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}
are contained in sub-level sets of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathsf{J}_{t}^{+,\varepsilon},\mathsf{J}_{t}^{-,\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{T}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{F}_{n}(\bar{\mathsf{P}})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathsf{P}^{\varepsilon},\mathsf{J}^{+,\varepsilon},\mathsf{J}^{-,\varepsilon})=0,

thus establishing the claim.
∎

3.3.
Variational characterization

We will now present the chain rule for the entropy. The strategy of the proof is similar to the mean-field case and the proof for jump processes of
[
PRST22
]
, with the difference that due to the unboundedness of
\bar{\kappa}
we need a two-fold regularization of the entropy, namely via truncations and compactly supported multipliers.

Theorem 3.21
.

For any
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
with
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})<\infty
, it holds that
t\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})
is absolutely continuous and

\frac{\mathrm{d}\,}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\bigl(\log U(\nu+\delta_{x})-\log U(\nu)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$.}

Moreover,
\mathcal{I}_{n}\geq 0
, and if
\mathcal{I}_{n}=0
we have

\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}=\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}\qquad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$.}

Proof.

For any curve
\mathsf{P}
with
\mathsf{P}\ll\Pi_{n}
for all
t\in[0,T]
we will use

S^{k,m}_{t}=:\int_{\Gamma}\phi_{k,m}(U_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n},\qquad S^{m}_{t}=:\int_{\Gamma}\phi_{m}(U_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n},

where
\phi_{k,m}(U,\nu)=\chi_{k}(\nu)\phi_{m}(U)
,
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\phi_{m}
the previously defined regularized entropy functions, and
\chi_{k}:=f_{k}(\nu(\mathcal{T}))\in C_{c}(\Gamma)
compactly supported multipliers defined via

f_{k}(z):=\left\{\begin{aligned} 1&,\qquad&&0\leq z\leq k,\\
2-\frac{z}{k}&,\qquad&&k\leq z\leq 2k,\\
0&,\qquad&&z\geq 2k.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Note that
|f_{k}|\leq 1
,
|f_{k}^{\prime}(z)|z\leq 2
uniformly in
k
,
f_{k}
converges monotonically to
1
, and
|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\xi_{k}|(\nu,x)\leq 3/(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))
if
k\geq 1
. In addition, recall that
\phi_{m}^{\prime}
converges pointwise to
\phi^{\prime}
and
|\phi^{\prime}_{m}|,\phi_{m}
converge monotonically to
|\phi^{\prime}|,\phi
respectively, and in particular,

\lim_{k\to\infty}S^{k,m}_{t}:=S^{m}_{t},\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}S^{m}_{t}=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}|\Pi_{n}).

Moreover, let the distributional derivatives with respect to
\mathsf{P}
be defined as

DS^{k,m}_{t}(\nu):=\chi_{k}(\nu)\phi^{\prime}_{m}(U_{t}(\nu)),\qquad DS^{m}_{t}(\nu):=\phi^{\prime}_{m}(U_{t}(\nu))

Note that pointwise
\lim_{k\to\infty}\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}DS^{k,m}_{t}=\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}DS^{m}_{t}
and
\lim_{m\to\infty}\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}DS^{m}_{t}=\overline{\nabla}^{n,\pm}\phi^{\prime}(U_{t})
.

Now, consider a curve
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
with
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}<\infty
. Since
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}
is bounded from below

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\qquad\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

and therefore
\mathsf{P}_{t}\ll\Pi_{n}
,
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}\ll\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}\ll\vartheta^{\pm}_{\Pi_{n}}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, with

\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\sqrt{U_{t}(\nu)U_{t}(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})}\,\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x).

In particular
U_{t}(\nu)
,
U_{t}(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})>0
for
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t},\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}
-a.e.
\nu,x
.

Moreover, set
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}=G_{t}^{\pm}\vartheta_{\Pi}^{\pm}
,
\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}=G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}\vartheta_{\Pi}^{+}
(or
G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}:=G_{t}^{+}-G_{t}^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,+})
, and

\displaystyle\ell:=(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\Pi_{n},\qquad\Sigma^{\pm}:=(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\Pi_{n}}.

By Lemma
3.15
, the map
t\mapsto U_{t}
is absolutely continuous and a.e. differentiable in
L^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma),\ell)
with

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U_{t}(\nu)

\displaystyle=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}(G_{t}^{-}(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)-G_{t}^{+}(\nu,x))\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle+n\int_{\mathcal{T}}(G_{t}^{+}(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)-G_{t}^{-}(\nu,x))\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x),

or in terms of the net-flux,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U_{t}(\nu)=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)-n\int_{\mathcal{T}}G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}(\nu,x)\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x).

Therefore, since
(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})
is bounded from above and below on the support of
\xi_{k}
, it is clear that for every
m,n
the maps
t\mapsto S_{t}^{k,m}
are Lipschitz, absolutely continuous and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\,}{\mathrm{d}t}S_{t}^{k,m}

\displaystyle=n\int_{\mathcal{T}}DS_{t}^{k,m}(\nu)G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\kappa^{-}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)-n\int_{\mathcal{T}}DS_{t}^{k,m}(\nu)G^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}(\nu,x)\,\kappa^{+}_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}DS_{t}^{k,m}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{t},

and in particular, for all
s,t\in[0,T]
,

(3.23)

S_{t}^{k,m}-S_{s}^{k,m}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}DS_{r}^{k,m}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}.

Recall that the following convergences hold pointwisely:

\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{k\to\infty}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}DS_{t}^{k,m}=\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U_{t}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to\infty}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\xi_{k}=0.

Moreover, the following estimate holds for every
(\nu,x)
:

\displaystyle|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}DS_{t}^{m}|(\nu,x)

\displaystyle\leq\|\xi_{k}\|_{\infty}|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}DS_{t}^{m}|(\nu,x)+\|\phi^{\prime}_{m}\|_{\infty}|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\xi_{k}|(\nu,x)

\displaystyle\leq|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}DS_{t}^{m}|(\nu,x)+3m(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U_{t})|(\nu,x)+3m(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{-1},

where the final inequality follows from the truncation inequality for discrete derivatives, i.e.
|\phi_{m}(\eta)-\phi_{m}(\nu)|\leq|\phi(\eta)-\phi(\nu)|
. Note that by Lemma
3.13
, for any
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{\pm}
with finite
\mathcal{R}_{n}
that

\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))^{-1}\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}|<\infty,

and moreover

\frac{1}{2n}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U)|\,\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}|\,\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2n}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U)\right)\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+},

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}^{-}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Psi^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2n}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U)\right)\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}

\displaystyle=\int_{U(\nu+n^{-1}\delta_{x})>0,U(\nu)>0}\Psi^{*}\left(\log U(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-U(\nu)\right)\sqrt{U(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})U(\nu)}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{\Pi}_{n}}^{+}

\displaystyle=\int_{U(\nu+n^{-1}\delta_{x})>0,U(\nu)>0}\left(\sqrt{U(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})}-\sqrt{U(\nu)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{+}_{\Pi_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}).

Therefore, since
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{0}|\Pi_{n})<\infty
we find by a dominated convergence argument and taking subsequent limits in
k
and
m
in (
3.23
) that
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}|\Pi_{n})<\infty
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t})-\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{s})

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad s,t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi(U_{t})|\,\mathrm{d}|\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{r}|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}),\qquad t\in[0,T].

and

\mathcal{I}_{n}\geq\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2n}\left(\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{T})-\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{0})\right)+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}^{-}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.

Next, assume that
\mathcal{I}_{n}=0
. Then the above arguments imply that for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.24)

\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,+\frac{1}{2n}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\mathrm{net}}_{t}+\mathcal{D}^{-}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})=0.

To simplify manipulations, let
U^{\pm}(\nu,x):=U\circ\mathsf{T}_{x}^{n,\pm}=U(\nu\pm\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})
. Note that for the actions,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\Theta^{n,+}_{\mathsf{P}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}1_{U,V>0}\,\phi\left(G^{+}U/U^{+}\right)\sqrt{UU^{+}}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{+},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{-}|\Theta^{n,-}_{\mathsf{P}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}1_{U,U^{-}>0}\,\phi\left(G^{-}U^{-}/U\right)\sqrt{UU^{-}}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{-}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}1_{U,U^{+}>0}\,\phi\left(G^{-}U^{+}/U\right)\sqrt{UU^{+}}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{+},

for the modified Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{n}^{-}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}^{-}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}1_{U,U^{+}>0}\left(\sqrt{U^{+}}-\sqrt{U}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{+}_{\Pi_{n}},

and finally

\frac{1}{2n}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}\phi^{\prime}(U)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))(G^{+}-G^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,-})\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{+},

which due to
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}\ll\Theta^{n,\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}
is equal to

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}1_{U,U^{+}>0}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U)(G_{t}^{+}-G_{t}^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,-})\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\Pi_{n}}^{+}.

Therefore, after some cumbersome rewriting, the integrands of the left-hand side of (
3.24
) reads as the indicator functions over
\{U,U^{+}>0\}
multiplied by the terms

\displaystyle\phi\left(G^{+}U/U^{+}\right)\sqrt{UU^{+}}+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))G_{t}^{+}+\phi^{*}\left(-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\;\phi\left(G^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,+}U^{+}/U\right)\sqrt{UU^{+}}-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))G^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,+}+\phi^{*}\left(-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))\right),

since

\phi^{*}\left(-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))\right)=U-\sqrt{UU^{+}},\quad\phi^{*}\left(\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}(\phi^{\prime}(U^{+})-\phi^{\prime}(U))\right)=U^{+}-\sqrt{UU^{+}}.

By duality of
\phi,\phi^{*}
we have
G^{+}=U
and
G^{-}\circ\mathsf{T}^{n,+}=U^{+}
, hence
G^{-}=U
as well. Subsequently we can conclude that
\mathcal{I}_{n}=0
if and only if
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}=\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
and a.e.
\nu,x
.
∎

Together, Theorems
3.21
and
3.20
provide a proof of the variational characterization for the forward Kolmogorov equation.

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Under the assumption of
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
we have by Theorem
3.21
a chain rule for the entropy, the inequality
\mathcal{I}_{n}\geq 0
, and the statement that
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=0
implies that
\mathsf{P}
is a weak solution. Moreover, due to Theorem
3.20
there exists a weak solution with
\mathcal{I}_{n}\leq 0
.

It remains to show that gradient-flow solutions are unique, which is a classical argument using the strict convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, e.g. see Theorem 5.9 of
[
PRST22
]
. Suppose that there exist two curves
\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2}
such that
\mathsf{P}^{1}_{0}=\mathsf{P}^{2}_{0}=\bar{\mathsf{P}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{1},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{1}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{1}}^{-})
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{2},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{2}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{2}}^{-})=0
. Applying the chain rule it is straightforward to verify that for a gradient-flow solution
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{t}=0
for every
t\in[0,T]
, where

\mathcal{I}_{n}^{t}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{r},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{r},\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})-\mathcal{F}_{n}(\bar{\mathsf{P}})+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r,

and that
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{t}\geq 0
for arbitrary curves with initial condition
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
.

Now, define
\tilde{\mathsf{P}}_{t}=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathsf{P}^{1}+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathsf{P}^{2}
and note that
(\tilde{\mathsf{P}},\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}}^{+},\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
as well, and

\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}}^{\pm}=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{1}}^{\pm}+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{2}}^{\pm}.

Fix any
t\in[0,T]
and suppose that
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{1}\neq\mathsf{P}_{t}^{2}
. Then by convexity of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
and
\mathcal{D}_{n}
, and strict convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}^{t}(\tilde{\mathsf{P}},\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}}^{+},\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}}^{-})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\tilde{\mathsf{P}}_{r},\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}_{r}}^{+},\vartheta_{\tilde{\mathsf{P}}_{r}}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathcal{F}_{n}(\tilde{\mathsf{P}}_{t})-\mathcal{F}_{n}(\bar{\mathsf{P}})+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle<\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{t}(\mathsf{P}^{1},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{1}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{1}}^{-})+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{t}(\mathsf{P}^{2},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{2}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{2}}^{-})=0,

which leads to a contradiction, and hence
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{1}=\mathsf{P}_{t}^{2}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

4.
Liouville equation and lifted dynamics

In this section, we will consider the variational formulation for our proposed limit of the forward Kolmogorov equation

FKE
n

, namely the
Liouville equation

(
Li
)

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}+\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma}\left(\mathsf{P}_{t}\bigl(\kappa^{+}-\kappa^{-}\bigr)\right)=0.

It can be interpreted as a transport equation lifted from the mean-field dynamics, in the sense that it describes the evolution of the law of a deterministic process satisfying the mean-field equation but with possibly random initial conditions. We will consider the same ingredients as in previous sections, namely a non-negative EDP functional consisting of an action term, a difference of free energies, and a corresponding Fisher information term. The main technical tool that we use is a new superposition principle, which allows us to prove the chain rule via the results on mean-field curves of Section
2
.

Solutions to (
Li
) are defined as appropriate weak solutions to

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}=Q_{\infty}^{*}\,\mathsf{P}_{t},

where
\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
for all
t\in[0,T]
and the operator
Q_{\infty}^{*}
is the dual of
Q_{\infty}
given by

\displaystyle(Q_{\infty}F)(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)(\nu,x)V[\nu](\mathrm{d}x),

\displaystyle V[\nu]

\displaystyle:=\kappa^{+}[\nu]-\kappa^{-}[\nu],

for all
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
. Here
\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
is the space of all compactly supported smooth cylinder functions, i.e. those of the form

F(\nu)=g\left(\langle 1,\nu\rangle,\langle f_{1},\nu\rangle,\dots,\langle f_{m},\nu\rangle\right),

where
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
, and
\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}
is the distributional gradient defined by

\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}\,F(\nu,x)=(\nabla g)\left(\langle 1,\nu\rangle,\langle f_{1},\nu\rangle,\dots,\langle f_{m},\nu\rangle\right)\cdot(1,f_{1}(x),\dots,f_{m}(x))^{\top}.

To be precise, we consider the following type of solutions.

Definition 4.1
.

A curve
(\mathsf{P}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a weak solution to (

Li

) if
\mathsf{P}_{t}
is continuous in the narrow topology and for all
s,t\in[0,T]
, and all
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
,

(4.1)

\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)(\nu,x)V[\nu](\mathrm{d}x)\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}r.

Remark 4.2
.

Note that (
Li
) is the transport equation associated to the measure-valued vector field
V[\nu]
. Now let the flow
G:[0,T]\times\Gamma\to\Gamma
be the unique strong solution to the mean-field equation, i.e. with

(4.2)

\partial_{t}G_{t}[\nu]=V[G_{t}[\nu]].

As will be shown in Section
4.2
,
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=(G_{t})_{\#}\bar{\mathsf{P}}
is a weak solution to (

Li

) for any initial data
\bar{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
. In particular, if
\nu_{t}
is a solution to (

MF

) than
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=\delta_{\nu_{t}}
is a weak solution to (
Li
).

Instead of the solution to (
Li
), we will now consider arbitrary curves satisfying

(
\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
)

\partial_{t}\mathsf{P}_{t}+\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma}(J_{t}^{+}-J_{t}^{-})=0,

in the following appropriate distributional sense.

Definition 4.3

(Continuity equation)
.

A triple
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
satisfies the continuity equation
\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
, if

(1)

the curve
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
is narrowly continuous,

(2)

the Borel family
(\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
satisfies

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

(3)

for every
s,t\in[0,T]
and all
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)

\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F\,(\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}-\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Moreover, let us introduce the EDP-functional. Recall from Section
3
the notation
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x):=\kappa^{\pm}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
be the geometric average of
\vartheta^{+}_{\mathsf{P}}
and
\vartheta^{-}_{\mathsf{P}}
, i.e.

\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x):=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{+}_{\mathsf{P}}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta^{-}_{\mathsf{P}}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

for any dominating measure
\Sigma
. We define the following objects:

•

The dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\times\mathcal{M}_{loc}^{+}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{+}|\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{-}|\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}}).

•

The dual dissipation potential
\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\infty}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\times\mathcal{B}_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}^{*}(\mathsf{P},\omega^{+},\omega^{-}):=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(e^{\omega^{+}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}}+\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(e^{\omega^{-}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}}.

•

The free energy
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}):=\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu)\,\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

•

The Fisher information
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}:\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)\to[0,+\infty]
,

\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}):=\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)\,\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

•

The
EDP-functional

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}:\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}\to[0,+\infty]
for all curves with
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
,

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}_{t}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\nu_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\nu_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Remark 4.5
.

Recall from Section
2
that
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu):=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma)
and

\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &2H^{2}(\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\kappa_{\nu}^{-}),&&\qquad\mbox{if $\nu\ll\gamma,$}\\
&+\infty,&&\qquad\mbox{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

In particular, if
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P})<\infty
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P})

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Gamma}H^{2}(\kappa_{\nu}^{+},\kappa_{\nu}^{-})\,\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=2H^{2}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-}).

Remark 4.6
.

Note that
\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\theta_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\infty})
is finite, we can set

\lambda^{\pm}_{t}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Theta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\infty}}(\nu,x)\,\theta_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}x),

and it is straightforward to verify that we have the disintegration

\mathsf{J}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\lambda_{t}^{\pm}[\nu](\mathrm{d}x)\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

and the equivalence

(4.3)

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}|\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}})=\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda_{t}^{\pm}[\nu]|\theta_{\nu})\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}.

Together with the definitions of
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
this implies that if
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
is finite then the
\lambda_{t}^{\pm}[\nu]
are well-defined for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, and

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t}[\nu],\lambda^{-}_{t}[\nu])+\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu)\right)\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu)\mathsf{P}_{T}(\mathrm{d}\nu)-\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu)\mathsf{P}_{0}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

Throughout the rest of this section we will simply write
\lambda_{t,\nu}^{\pm}=\lambda^{\pm}_{t}[\nu]
.

We will show the following equivalence, which subsumes Theorem (
1.7
).

Theorem 4.7
.

For any
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
with
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
, the EDP-functional
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
is finite if and only if there exists a Borel probability measure
Q
over
C([0,T];\Gamma)
such that

(1)

for the time-evaluations
e_{t}
we have
(e_{t})_{\#}Q=\mathsf{P}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(2)

the measure
Q
is concentrated on the family of curves
\nu\in AC([0,T];(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV}))
such that
(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{\nu},\lambda^{-}_{\nu})\in\mathscr{CE}
, where
\lambda_{\nu}^{\pm}
is defined via the disintegration

\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\lambda_{t,\nu}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\qquad\text{for a.e. $t\in[0,T]$,}

(3)

we have the representation

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=\int\mathcal{I}_{MF}\left(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{\nu},\lambda^{-}_{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q,

with the latter term finite.

In particular,
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\geq 0
, and

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=0\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} \quad&\mbox{$\mathsf{P}_{t}$\; is the weak solution to \eqref{eq:liouv2} with $\mathsf{P}_{t}=(G_{t})_{\#}\mathsf{P}_{0}$}\\
\quad\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}&=\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}\quad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Here
G_{t}:\Gamma\to\Gamma
maps
\bar{\nu}
to the unique mean-field solution
\nu_{t}
at time
t
, see Remark
4.2
. It is determined by

\partial_{t}G_{t}[\nu]=V[G_{t}[\nu]].

We do not have a priori uniqueness of the Liouville equation. However, we do have uniqueness of weak solutions for which a superposition holds, in particular for curves with finite
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
. Therefore gradient-flow solutions (null-minimizers of
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
) are in fact unique.

In the case of
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=\delta_{\nu_{t}}
with
\nu_{t}
the solution to the mean-field equation there is a trivial superposition principle, and we have the following consequence.

Corollary 4.8
.

Suppose
\mathsf{P}_{0}=\delta_{\nu_{0}}
with
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
. Then

\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=0\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} \quad&\mbox{$\mathsf{P}_{t}=\delta_{\nu_{t}}$,\hskip 10.22217pt$\nu_{t}$ is the unique strong solution to \eqref{eq:mf}}\quad\\
\quad&\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}=\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}\quad\mbox{for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}\quad\end{aligned}\right.

4.1.
A priori estimates

Due to the representation (
4.3
) of the dissipation potential in terms of mean-field objects, we can directly derive the following estimates from Lemma’s
2.9
and
2.12
.

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma),\mathsf{J}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
be such that
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})<\infty
, and set

\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{\nu}:=\lambda^{+}_{\nu}-\lambda^{-}_{\nu}.

Then the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}M\phi\left(\frac{\lambda_{\nu}^{\pm}(\mathcal{T})}{M(1+\nu(X)^{2})}\vee 1\right)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}),

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}M\Psi\left(\frac{\|\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{\nu}\|_{TV}}{M(1+\nu(X))}\right)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}).

Moreover, the following equivalence follows straightforwardly from Lemma
3.13
.

Corollary 4.10
.

For any
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma),\mathsf{J}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{J}^{\pm}|\Theta^{\infty}_{\mathsf{P}})=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\right)\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

for any common dominating measure
\Sigma
.

Finally, we consider the time-regularity for arbitrary curves, with respect to the following metric.

Definition 4.11
.

We define the following metric:

(4.4)

W(\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2}):=\sup_{F\in\mathbb{F}}\left\{\int_{\Gamma}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}^{1}-\mathsf{P}^{2})\right\},\qquad\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma),

where

\mathbb{F}:=\left\{F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)\,:\,\left\|(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}.

Note that
W
is narrowly lower semicontinuous. Moreover, for any
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
automatically
\left\|(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}\,F\right\|_{\infty}<\infty
, and hence by a density argument it is straightforward to verify that convergence in
W
implies vague convergence on
\Gamma
, and therefore narrow convergence on narrowly pre-compact subsets.

Remark 4.12
.

Formally, one can represent
W
as a transport distance, in the sense that

W(\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2})=W_{d_{\Gamma}}(\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2}),

where
W_{d_{\Gamma}}
is the 1-Wasserstein metric on
\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
induced by the metric
d_{\Gamma}
over
\Gamma
given by

d_{\Gamma}(\nu^{1},\nu^{2}):=\inf_{(\nu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}}\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|\dot{\nu}_{t}|_{TV}}{1+\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\,:\,\nu_{0}=\nu^{0},\,\nu_{1}=\nu^{2}\right\}.

However, we do not require such representations in this current work.

Lemma 4.13
.

For any
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
we have

W(\mathsf{P}_{s},\mathsf{P}_{t})\leq 2\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+\mathsf{J}^{-}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mbox{ for all }s,t\in[0,T].

Proof.

This follows directly from the continuity equation, since for any
F\in\mathbb{F}
,
s,t\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\left|(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F\right|(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Taking the supremum over all
F\in\mathbb{F}
we obtain the desired statement.
∎

4.2.
Weak solutions

Here we briefly consider existence and representations for solutions to the Liouville equation.

Lemma 4.14
.

For any
\bar{\mathsf{P}}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
there exists a solution
\mathsf{P}
to (
Li
) with initial data
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

Recall the flow
G:[0,T]\times\Gamma\to\Gamma
determined by

\partial_{t}G_{t}[\nu]=V[G_{t}[\nu]],

Set
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=(G_{t})_{\#}\bar{\mathsf{P}}
. We will show that
\mathsf{P}_{t}
is weak solution in the sense of (
4.1
). Namely, consider any
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
. Due the strong regularity of solutions to the mean-field equation it is straightforward to show that for all
s,t\in[0,T]
we have the chain rule

F\circ G_{t}(\nu)-F(\nu)=\int_{s}^{t}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)(G_{r}\circ\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}V[G_{r}\circ\nu]\,\mathrm{d}r,

and hence

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{s}^{t}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)(G_{r}\circ\nu,x)\,V[G_{r}[\nu]](\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\bar{\mathsf{P}}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)(\nu,x)V[\nu](\mathrm{d}x)\mathsf{P}_{r}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}t,

and thus
\mathsf{P}_{t}
is indeed a weak solution.
∎

4.3.
Superposition principle

One of our main tools in proving the chain rule, uniqueness of solutions, and the variational representation of Theorem
4.7
is the superposition principle. It guarantees that we can represent the action as an expectation of the mean-field action under some measure over curves in
\mathscr{CE}
, and allows us to use the theory on mean-field dynamics of Section
2
. In this section, we will make this notion precise.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
with

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Then there exists a Borel probability measure
Q\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\Gamma))
satisfying
(e_{t})_{\#}Q=\mathsf{P}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and concentrated on curves
\nu\in AC([0,T];(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV}))
, for which
(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{\nu},\lambda^{-}_{\nu})\in\mathscr{CE}
.
Moreover,

(4.5)

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}\left(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu).

Conversely, if there is a Borel probability measure
Q\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\Gamma))
concentrated on curves
\nu\in AC([0,T];(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV}))
and a Borel family
\{\lambda^{\pm}_{t,\nu}\}
, for which
(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{\nu},\lambda^{-}_{\nu})\in\mathscr{CE}
, with

\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}\left(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu)<\infty,

then
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
for
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\#}Q
,
\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}:=\mathsf{P}_{t}\lambda^{\pm}_{t,\nu}
, and (
4.5
) holds as well.

The inspiration for using a superposition principle stems from similar approaches in
[
EFLS16
]
,
[
Erb16
]
, where it is applied to transport equations lifted from the Boltzmann-equation or mean-field jump dynamics respectively, and the main ingredient is the abstract superposition principle over
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
of
[
AT14
]
. However, these results are not directly applicable to our setting, since the mass of
\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})
for a mean-field curve is not fixed, and
V[\nu](\mathcal{T})
is finite but unbounded over
\Gamma
. We remedy this by combining two known superposition principles: on the one hand, the abstract superposition principle over
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
of
[
AT14
]
, and on the other hand one for finite-dimensional vector fields with linear growth, found in
[
AC08
]
. Our result is stated in Theorem
B.1
.

Proof.

Consider any
