(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
with finite
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
, and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
set
\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t,\nu}:=\lambda_{t}^{+}-\lambda^{-}_{t}
. By Corollary
4.9
,

(4.6)

\int_{\Gamma}M\Psi\left(\frac{\|\lambda^{\mathrm{net}}_{t,\nu}\|_{TV}}{M(1+\nu(\mathcal{T}))}\right)\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},J_{t}^{-}).

Now, take a countable and dense set
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
, with
f_{1}=1
,
\|f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,
i\geq 2
, and define
\mathbb{T}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}

\mathbb{T}(\nu):=\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu,\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu\ldots\right).

Note that
\mathbb{T}(\nu)
is injective, continuous when
\Gamma
is equipped with the narrow topology and
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
with product topology, and is an isometry between
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV})
and
(\mathbb{T}(\Gamma),|\cdot|_{\infty})
, where
|\cdot|_{\infty}
is the uniform norm over
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We set
\sigma_{t}:=\mathbb{T}_{\#}\mathsf{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
, and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
define the vector field
\mathbf{W}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
via its components

W_{i}(t,z):=\int_{X}f_{i}(x)\,\lambda_{t,\mathbb{T}^{-1}(z)}(\mathrm{d}x).

Note that the support of
\mathbf{W}_{t}
is in
\mathbb{T}(\Gamma)
, that
|\mathbf{W}_{t}(z)|_{\infty}\leq\|\lambda_{t,\mathbb{T}^{-1}(z)}\|_{TV}
and
(\mathbb{T}(\nu))_{1}=\nu(\mathcal{T})
. Therefore,
by (
4.6
) we have the estimate

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}M\Psi\left(\frac{|\mathbf{W}_{t}(z)|_{\infty}}{M(1+|z_{1}|)}\right)\sigma(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Moreover,
(\sigma,\mathbf{W})
satisfy the continuity equation, in the sense that for all
g\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}g\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{t}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}g\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}(\mathbf{W}_{r},\nabla g)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\qquad\text{for every $s,t\in[0,T]$.}

Indeed, take any
g\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
and define
F:=g\circ\mathbb{T}
, i.e.

F(\nu)=g\left(\langle f_{1},\nu\rangle,\dots,\langle f_{m},\nu\rangle\right).

Note that
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
, and therefore since
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}g(z)\,\sigma_{t}(\mathrm{d}z)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}g(z)\,\sigma_{s}(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}\,F)(\nu,x)(\mathsf{J}^{+}_{r}-\mathsf{J}^{-}_{r})(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\sum_{i}(\partial_{i}g)(\mathbb{T}(\nu))\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}(x)\lambda_{r,\nu}(\mathrm{d}x)\right)\mathsf{P}_{r}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}\nabla g(z)\cdot\mathbf{W}_{r}(z)\,\sigma_{r}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\mathrm{d}r.

Thus, we are now in a position to apply Theorem
B.1
, and obtain a Borel probability measure
\Omega
over
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
satisfying
(e_{t})_{\#}\Omega=\sigma_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and which is concentrated on the family of curves
z\in AC([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
that are solutions to the ODE

\dot{z}_{t}=\mathbf{W}_{t}(z_{t})\qquad\text{for almost every $t\in[0,T]$.}

Note that since
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathbb{T}(\Gamma)
, we have
\mathrm{supp}(\Omega)\subseteq AC([0,T];\mathbb{T}(\Gamma))
. Now let

\tilde{\mathbb{T}}:C([0,T];\Gamma)\to C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
be defined via
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}(\nu))_{t}:=\mathbb{T}(\nu_{t})
. Similar as for
\mathbb{T}
,
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
is injective and an isometry when seen as a map
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}:AC([0,T];(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV}))\to AC([0,T];(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}},|\cdot|_{\infty}))
. Therefore, it is clear the measure
Q:=\tilde{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}_{\#}\Omega\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\Gamma))
is well defined, satisfies
\mathsf{P}_{t}=(e_{t})_{\#}Q
and is concentrated on the family of curves
\nu\in AC([0,T];(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV}))
, for which

\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\lambda^{+}_{r,\nu}-\lambda^{+}_{r,\nu})\,\mathrm{d}r\qquad\mbox{for all $s,t\in[0,T]$, $i\in\mathbb{N}$}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}\left(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu})\,\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

where the latter is finite by assumption, and hence, by Lemma
2.13
, we deduce that
(\nu,\lambda_{\nu}^{+},\lambda_{\nu}^{+})\in\mathscr{CE}

Q
-almost everywhere.

The reverse statement can be derived straightforwardly and we omit the proof.
∎

4.4.
Variational characterization

Having all the ingredients at hand, we can now prove the variational characterization for the Liouville equation, namely Theorem
4.7
.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
is such that
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
and
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}<\infty
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
is non-negative we have in particular that

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},J_{t}^{+},J_{t}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\quad\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{T})<\infty,\quad\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Hence, from the superposition principle of Theorem
4.15
, we obtain a Borel probability measure
Q
over
C([0,T];\Gamma)
satisfying
(e_{t})_{\#}Q=\mathsf{P}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and concentrated on the family of curves
\nu\in AC([0,T];(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{TV}))
for which
(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{\nu},\lambda^{-}_{\nu})\in\mathscr{CE}
. Moreover,

\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}\left(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}t\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu)=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
we have that for
Q
-a.e. curve
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
. Moreover, since both
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
are simply their mean-field counterparts integrated by
\mathsf{P}
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\mathcal{I}_{MF}\left(\nu,\lambda^{+}_{\nu},\lambda_{\nu}^{-}\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\,=\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}\left(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{t})-\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\,=\int_{C([0,T];\Gamma)}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{MF}\left(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t,\nu},\lambda^{-}_{t,\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}t\right)Q(\mathrm{d}\nu)+\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\,=\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}),

where the second equality follows from Fubini-Tonelli and the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{MF},\mathcal{D}_{MF},\mathcal{F}_{MF}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
. In particular, by the non-negativeness of
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\geq 0
.

Moverover, since
\mathcal{I}_{MF}=0
if and only if
\nu
is the unique strong solution for an initial datum
\bar{\nu}
with
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\nu}|\gamma)<\infty
, we derive by non-negativeness of
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
that
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}=0
if and only if
Q
is concentrated on the unique solutions of the mean-field equation. In this case
Q
is characterized by

Q=\tilde{G}_{\#}\mathsf{P}_{0},

where
G_{t}:\Gamma\to\Gamma
defined by (
4.2
) maps any
\bar{\nu}
to the unique solution to (

MF

) for initial condition
\nu_{0}=\bar{\nu}
and
\tilde{G}:\Gamma\to C([0,T],\Gamma)
is defined via
(\tilde{G}(\nu_{0}))_{t}:=G_{t}(\nu_{0})
. Note that
\mathsf{P}_{t}=(G_{t})_{\#}\mathsf{P}_{0}
,
\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}=\mathsf{P}_{t}\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
, and in particular
\mathsf{P}_{t}
is a weak solution to (
Li
).

Vice versa, if
\mathsf{P}
is a weak solution such that
\mathsf{P}_{t}=(G_{t})_{\#}\mathsf{P}_{0}
, we simply set

Q:=\tilde{G}_{\#}\mathsf{P}_{0},\qquad\lambda_{\nu}^{\pm}:=\kappa_{\nu}^{\pm}\quad\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Since
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})<\infty
, we still have
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma)<\infty
for
\mathsf{P}_{0}
-almost every
\nu
, and we repeat the same calculations to conclude that indeed
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}=0
.
∎

5.
EDP convergence

In the previous sections, we have established variational formulations for the solution to the forward Kolmogorov equation of the interacting particle system, for the solutions to the mean-field equation, and the corresponding Liouville equation. Moreover, for the latter, we have shown how the corresponding EDP-functional can be represented as the expectation over a functional of mean-field paths.

We are now in a position to rigorously discuss the convergence of the forward Kolmogorov equation to the Liouville equation, in terms of EDP-convergence of their gradient structures.
Namely, let us denote a sequence of curves
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
converging to a curve
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
, denoted by
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})
, if the following holds:

•

\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}\to\mathsf{P}_{t}
narrowly for all
t\in[0,T]
,

•

\mathsf{J}_{t}^{n,\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t\to\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t
vaguely on
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}\times[0,T])
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, is such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})<\infty,\qquad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})<\infty,

then the family of curves
\{(\mathsf{P}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\}_{n}
is W-equicontinuous (
4.4
), and there exists a (not relabelled) subsequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})
and a
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}),

Moreover, for any such converging sequence

(5.1)

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t}),\qquad\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{n,-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Remark 5.2
.

In fact, the compactness result is slightly stronger. As shown in the proof of Theorem
5.1
the measures
\mathsf{J}_{r}^{n,\pm}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}r
converge vaguely on
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}\times[s,t])
for any
s,t\in[0,T]
.

Note that if in addition the initial data is well-prepared, in the sense that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0}),

then for any converging subsequence, we clearly have the liminf-estimate

(5.2)

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\geq\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}),

or in other words, obtain evolutionary
\varGamma
-convergence of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
.

Now, recall by Theorem
3.8
that unique gradient-flow solutions to the forward Kolmogorov equations (

FKE
n

) exist, and similarly, gradient-flow solutions to the Liouville equation (
Li
) are unique by Theorem
4.7
. Therefore, modifying classical arguments from
[
SS04
,
Ser11
]
, we can directly conclude the following convergence for the sequence of solutions.

Theorem 5.3
.

Consider a converging sequence
\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{n})\ni\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}\to\bar{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
such that

(5.3)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n})=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}),

and for each
n\geq 0
let
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}
be the unique gradient-flow solution to
(

FKE
n

)
with initial data
\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}
. Then there exists a unique gradient-flow solution
\mathsf{P}
to (
Li
) with initial data
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
. Moreover, we have the convergence

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{-})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{P},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t}),\qquad\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{-})=0
for all
n\geq 0
. Therefore, by (
5.3
) and Theorem
5.1
we have for any subsequence indexed by
n^{\prime}
converging to a
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
that (
5.2
) holds, and hence

\displaystyle 0=\liminf_{n^{\prime}\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n^{\prime}},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n^{\prime}}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n^{\prime}}}^{-})\geq\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}),

and thus
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})=0
, which implies that
\mathsf{P}
is the unique gradient-flow solution to (
Li
) and
\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}=\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. The convergence of
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}
now follows from a compactness and equicontinuity argument, and by lower semicontinuity we conclude that for every
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})-\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{-})+\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-})+\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t}).

∎

Now suppose that in addition the initial sequence of measures
\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}
is chaotic, in the sense that

\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}\to\delta_{\bar{\nu}}\quad\text{narrowly for some $\bar{\nu}\in\Gamma$.}

Then as a consequence of Theorem
5.3
we have propagation of chaos, namely

\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}\to\delta_{\bar{\nu}_{t}}\quad\text{narrowly}\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T],

where
\nu_{t}
is the unique solution to the mean-field equation (
2.13
) with initial datum
\bar{\nu}
. As mentioned in the introduction, while for interacting particle systems with the number of particles fixed at
n\in\mathbb{N}
this would imply narrow convergence of the
k
-marginals at time
t
to
\nu_{t}^{\otimes k}
(e.g. see
[
Szn91
]
), in our setting this implies convergence of the
k
-correlation functions
[
BGSRS20
]
.

Moreover, note that we have a stronger notion of convergence, since the free energies
\mathcal{F}_{n}
converge as well. Under appropriate conditions on the initial datum
\bar{\nu}
, this guarantees a version of propagation of entropic chaoticity. Namely, for any
\nu
we define the rescaled Poisson measures

\Pi_{n,\nu}:=(L_{n})_{\#}\pi_{n,\nu},\qquad\text{where}\qquad\pi_{n,\nu}:=\frac{1}{e^{n\nu(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\nu^{\otimes N}.

It is straightforward to check that
\Pi_{n,\nu^{*}}\to\delta_{\nu^{*}}
narrowly. We then have the following result.

Theorem 5.4

(Propagation of chaos)
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
5.3
and assume additionally that
\bar{\mathsf{P}}=\delta_{\bar{\nu}}
for some
\bar{\nu}\in\Gamma
with
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\nu}|\gamma)<\infty
. Let
\nu_{t}
be the unique solution to (
2.13
) with initial datum
\bar{\nu}
. Then for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}\to\delta_{\nu_{t}}\quad\text{narrowly},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}|\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma).

If additionally there exists a constant
C>1
such that
C^{-1}\leq\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}/\mathrm{d}\gamma\leq C
then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}|\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}})=0,\qquad\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Theorems
5.1
and
5.4
are proved in Section
5.3
. However, first we show
\Gamma
-convergence of the free energies in Section
5.1
, and establish the necessary estimates in Section
5.2
.

5.1.
\varGamma
-convergence of
\mathcal{F}_{n}

While only the liminf-estimates for the free energy
\mathcal{F}_{n}
are necessary for the proof of Theorem
5.1
and the convergence of solutions, we provide here the full
\varGamma
-convergence result. We rely strongly on the characterization of
[
Mar12
]
, which connects a large deviation principle with rate function
I
to the fact that

\mathop{\varGamma\text{-lim}}_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi^{n})=\int_{\Gamma}I(\nu)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

and provides useful sufficient conditions for both.

Recall in our setting that

\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P})=\frac{1}{2n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi_{n}),\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma).

We then have the following result, which we prove after Lemma
5.6
below.

Theorem 5.5
.

The family
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is equicoercive and
\varGamma
-converges to
\mathcal{F}
in the sense that

•

for any converging sequence
\mathsf{P}^{n}\to\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
:

\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\nu)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}),

•

for any
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
with
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P})<\infty
there exists a sequence
\mathsf{P}^{n}\in\Gamma
converging to
\mathsf{P}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n})=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}).

By the results of
[
Mar12
, Theorems 3.4, 3.5]
it is sufficient to merely show the corresponding bounds or limits for any
\mathsf{P}
of the form
\mathsf{P}=\delta_{\nu}
for some
\nu\in\Gamma
. Because of this reduction, we can make use of the so-called cumulant generating functionals
G_{n}
given by

G_{n}(f):=\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{\Gamma}e^{n\langle f,\nu\rangle}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

for any
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\Gamma)
, and their limit counterpart

G(f):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}(e^{f}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma.

Note that by duality of the entropy, we have for all
n>0
the inequality

(5.4)

\int_{\Gamma}\langle f,\nu\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}\leq\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi_{n})+G_{n}(f),\quad

and for the Legendre-dual of
G
we have

G^{*}(\nu):=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathcal{T})}\bigl\{\langle f,\nu\rangle-G(f)\bigr\}=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\gamma|\nu).

We will first simplify
G_{n}
and show that it indeed converges to
G
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T})
. Then for each
n>0

G_{n}(f)=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\log\frac{e^{n\int_{\mathcal{T}}e^{f}\mathrm{d}\gamma}-1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}.

In particular

\lim_{n\to\infty}G_{n}(f)=G(f).

Proof.

Using the representation for the rescaled Poisson measure
\Pi_{n}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}e^{n\langle f,\nu\rangle}\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(x_{i})}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{n^{N}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}}e^{f}\mathrm{d}\gamma\right)^{n}}{N!}=\frac{e^{n\int_{\mathcal{T}}e^{f}\mathrm{d}\gamma}-1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1},

and after taking logarithms and dividing by
n
we obtain the desired statement. Moreover, recall that by assumption
\gamma(\mathcal{T})>0
and note that by the boundedness of
f
,

0<\int_{\mathcal{T}}e^{f}\mathrm{d}\gamma<\infty.

Hence we can take limit
n\to\infty
to deduce

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}G_{n}(f)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\left(e^{n\int_{\mathcal{T}}e^{f}\mathrm{d}\gamma}-1\right)-\frac{1}{n}\log\left(e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}}e^{f}\mathrm{d}\gamma-\gamma(\mathcal{T})=G(f),

thereby concluding the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

First, we will show that the family
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is equicoercive, by establishing a first moment bound for
\mathsf{P}
in terms of mass
\nu(\mathcal{T})
. Namely, setting
f=1
in (
5.4
) we have for any
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
,
n\geq 1
, the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\nu(\mathcal{T})\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}|\Pi_{n})+G_{n}(1)\leq 2\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P})+\frac{1}{n}\log\frac{e^{ne\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1},

where the final term is bounded from above independently of
\mathsf{P}
.

Next, for the limit inferior, consider a converging sequence
\mathsf{P}^{n}\to\mathsf{P}=\delta_{\bar{\nu}}
for some
\bar{\nu}\in\Gamma
. Fix any
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
, then by the duality (
5.4
),

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}|\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma}{\langle f,\nu\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}-\limsup_{n\to\infty}G_{n}(f)=\langle f,\bar{\nu}\rangle-G(f).

Taking the supremum over all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{T})
we find

\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\delta_{\bar{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\nu}|\gamma)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}).

Finally, consider any
\bar{\nu}\in\Gamma
with
\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\nu}|\gamma)<\infty
and set
\mathsf{P}=\delta_{\bar{\nu}}
. We will construct a sequence of measures
\mathsf{P}^{n}
that locally consists of Poisson measures induced by
\bar{\nu}
. Namely, set

\Pi_{n,\bar{\nu}}:=(L_{n})_{\#}\pi_{n,\bar{\nu}},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\pi_{n,\bar{\nu}}:=\frac{1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\bar{\nu}^{\otimes N},

and consider the sequence
\mathsf{P}^{n}:=\Pi_{n,\bar{\nu}}
. It is straightforward to verify that indeed
\mathsf{P}^{n}\to\delta_{\bar{\nu}}
. Moreover, note that although
L_{n}
is not bijective, we do have the equality

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}|\Pi_{n})=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\pi_{n,\bar{\nu}}|\pi_{n}),

due to the symmetry of the
N
-particle distributions
\bar{\nu}^{\otimes N}
,
\gamma^{\otimes N}
. Therefore, we derive

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}|\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\pi_{n,\bar{\nu}}|\pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\log\left(\frac{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}^{\otimes N}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}^{\otimes N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\left(N\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})^{N-1}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}+\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})^{N}\log\frac{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=n\frac{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}+\log\frac{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}.

Rescaling and taking the limit
n\to\infty
, we obtain

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}|\Pi_{n})=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}-\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})+\gamma(\mathcal{T})=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\bar{\nu}|\gamma),

therewith concluding the proof.
∎

5.2.
Uniform estimates

In Section
3.1
we provided uniform-in-
n
estimates for the flux. Namely, from Lemma
3.13
, we directly have the following.

Corollary 5.7
.

Consider a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{n,-}_{t})<\infty.

Then

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}3M\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{1}{3M}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(X)^{2})^{-1}\,\mathsf{J}^{n,\pm}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

where
M:=(1+\gamma(\mathcal{T}))\|c\|_{\infty}
.

However, the weighted total variation metric
d_{TV,w}
that was introduced is not appropriate for taking limits, and instead, we take the weaker metric defined in (
4.4
),

W(\mathsf{P}^{1},\mathsf{P}^{2}):=\sup_{F\in\mathbb{F}}\left\{\int_{\Gamma}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}^{1}-\mathsf{P}^{2})\right\},

where

\mathbb{F}:=\left\{F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)\,:\,\left\|(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})\,\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}\,F\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}.

Recall that
W
is narrowly lower semicontinuous and implies narrow convergence on narrowly pre-compact subsets.
We now have the follow equicontinuity result.

Lemma 5.8
.

Consider a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{n,-}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Then

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{|\dot{\mathsf{P}}^{n}_{t}|_{W}}{12M}\right)<\infty,

where
|\dot{\mathsf{P}}_{t}|_{W}
is the
W
-metric speed and
\tilde{\phi}(s):=\phi(s\vee 1)
is the monotone relaxation of
\phi
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Lemmas
3.15
and
4.13
, now for the distance
W
instead of the weighted total variation metric
d_{TV,w}
. Namely, fix
n>0
and consider a curve
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
. Then we have for any
s,t\in[0,T]
and any
F\in C_{c}(\Gamma)
,

\left|\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}_{t}-\mathsf{P}_{s})\right|\leq\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F(\nu,x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}|\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F(\nu,x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}r.

Substituting any
F\in\mathbb{F}
it is straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle|\overline{\nabla}^{n,+}F(\nu,x)|=n|F(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle|\overline{\nabla}^{n,-}F(\nu,x)|=n|F(\nu)-F(\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})|

\displaystyle\leq(1+(\nu(\mathcal{T})-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n})^{2})^{-1}\leq 2(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1},

for sufficiently large
n
, and therefore

\left|\int_{\Gamma}F\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{P}_{t}-\mathsf{P}_{s})\right|\leq 2\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}+\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Taking the supremum over
F\in\mathbb{F}
, we find that
(\mathsf{P}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
W
with

|\dot{\mathsf{P}}_{t}|_{W}\leq 2\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(\mathcal{T})^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{J}_{t}^{+}+\mathsf{J}_{t}^{-})\qquad\mbox{for a.e. $t\in[0,T]$},

where
|\dot{\mathsf{P}}^{n}_{t}|_{W}
is the
W
-metric speed. Applying the estimates in Lemma
3.13
concludes the proof.
∎

5.3.
Proof of main results

We finally conclude the manuscript with the proof of the main results.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We will first establish the liminf-estimates. Namely, consider a sequence

(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
that converges to the curve
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
. In particular
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}\to\mathsf{P}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and hence by Theorem
5.5
on the
\Gamma
-convergence of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
we immediately obtain

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n})\geq\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t}),\qquad\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Now suppose that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})<\infty,\qquad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})<\infty.

In particular we have the bounds

(5.5)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Due to the chain rule and the assumption on
\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{0})
, we obtain

(5.6)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t})<\infty.

The latter guarantees, by Corollary
C.3
, that we have the vague convergence

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}}=\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}},\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}}^{\pm}=\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}.

Recall that from Lemma
3.13
and Remark
3.6
we have for each
n\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}\left(\mathsf{J}_{t}^{n,\pm}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{n,+}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{t}^{n,\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\mp})}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\right)\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t})

\displaystyle=2H^{2}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm},\mathsf{T}^{n,\mp}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\mp}),

for any dominating measure
\Sigma
, and similarly, from Corollary
4.10
and Remark
4.5
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}\left(\mathsf{J}_{t}^{\pm}|\Theta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\Upsilon\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma},\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\mp}}{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}\right)\mathrm{d}\Sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t})

\displaystyle=2H^{2}(\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\pm},\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}_{t}}^{\mp}).

By the convexity and lower semi-continuity of
\Upsilon
and
H
we conclude by standard semi-continuity results (e.g. see
[
But89
, Theorem 3.4.3]
) that for each
t\in[0,T]
,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{n,-}_{t})\geq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t},\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t}),\qquad\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n})\geq\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t}),

from which (
5.1
) directly follows after applying the Fatou lemma.

Next, we consider the question of compactness. As in the previous part, let us consider a sequence
(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{n}
with

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{0}^{n})<\infty,\qquad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n},\mathsf{J}^{n,+},\mathsf{J}^{n,-})<\infty,

which imply that the estimates (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) still hold. The bound on the free energy ensures by Theorem
5.5
that
\{\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}\}_{t\in[0,T],n\geq 1}
is pre-compact. Moreover, due to the bound on the action
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, we have by the results of Corollary (
5.7
) and Lemma (
5.8
) that

(5.7)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{1}{3M}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(X)^{2})^{-1}\,\mathsf{J}^{n,\pm}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

(5.8)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{|\dot{\mathsf{P}}^{n}_{t}|_{W}}{12M}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

where
|\dot{\mathsf{P}}^{n}_{t}|_{W}
is again the
W
-metric speed. From (
5.7
), we then conclude from the non-decreasing, convex and super-linear at infinity property of
\tilde{\phi}
that, up to choosing a subsequence
n^{\prime}
, there exists a family
\{\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}^{+}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
such that for all
s,t
the sequence of measures
\mathsf{J}^{n^{\prime},\pm}_{r}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}r
converges to
\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{r}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}r
in
\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}\times[s,t])
, and

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}\tilde{\phi}\left(\frac{1}{3M}\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(1+\nu(X)^{2})^{-1}\,\mathsf{J}^{\pm}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Similarly, since the metric
W
is narrowly lower semicontinuous and induces narrow convergence on narrowly pre-compact subsets, we find by an Arzela-Ascoli argument and the estimate (
5.8
) that, up to choosing a subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}
, there exist a narrowly continuous curve
(\mathsf{P}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
such that
\mathsf{P}^{n^{\prime\prime}}_{t}
converges to
\mathsf{P}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

All that remains is showing that
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\in\mathsf{CE}_{\infty}
. Therefore, fix any
s,t\in[0,T]
and
F\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\Gamma)
. It is straightforward to verify that there exist constants
K_{F}
and
C_{F}
such that the following Taylor approximation holds:

\left|\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}(\nu,x)\mp n\left(F(\nu{\pm}\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x})-F(\nu)\right)\right|\leq\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{C_{F}}{n}1_{\nu(\mathcal{T})\leq K_{F}}(\nu,x),\qquad\mbox{ for all }\nu\in\Gamma,\,x\in\mathcal{T}.

Thus, we can take the limit in the continuity equation
\mathsf{CE}_{n}
, to conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n^{\prime\prime}}_{t}-\int_{\Gamma}F(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n^{\prime\prime}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{s}^{t}\left(\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\overline{\nabla}^{n^{\prime\prime},+}F)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{n^{\prime\prime},+}+(\overline{\nabla}^{n^{\prime\prime},-}F)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{n^{\prime\prime},-}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\Big(\int_{\Gamma\times\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{+}-(\mathrm{grad}_{\Gamma}F)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{J}_{r}^{-}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r,

thereby concluding the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Suppose that
\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}\to\bar{\mathsf{P}}=\delta_{\bar{\nu}}
with

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma).

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}
be the unique gradient-flow solution to
(

FKE
n

)
with initial data
\bar{\mathsf{P}}^{n}
. Moreover, let
\nu_{t}
be the unique solution to (
2.13
) with initial data
\bar{\nu}
, and set
\mathsf{P}_{t}:=\delta_{\nu_{t}}
, which is the unique gradient-flow solution to the Liouville equation (
Li
) with initial data
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then by Theorem
5.3
we have for every
t\in[0,T]
that
\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}\to\mathsf{P}_{t}
, and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t})=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\mathsf{P}_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma).

Next, suppose that in addition there exists a constant
C>1
such that
C^{-1}\leq\mathrm{d}\bar{\nu}/\mathrm{d}\gamma\leq C
. By Lemma
2.17
we find that there exists
C^{\prime}<\infty
with

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\log u_{t}\right\|_{\infty}<C^{\prime},\qquad u_{t}:=\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}/\mathrm{d}\gamma.

Now fix any
t\in[0,T]
, and recall that

\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}:=(L_{n})_{\#}\pi_{n,\nu_{t}},\qquad\pi_{n,\nu_{t}}=\frac{1}{e^{n\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{N}}{N!}\nu_{t}^{\otimes N}.

It is straightforward to check that
\Pi_{n}\ll\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}\ll\Pi_{n}
and hence for any
\Gamma_{n}\ni\Gamma_{n}=L_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
,

\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}}\right)(\nu)=\log\left(\frac{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}^{\otimes N}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log u_{t}(x_{i}),

with all terms finite, and
|\sum\log u_{t}(x_{i})|\leq NC^{\prime}
. Therefore, by applying a similar density argument for
\log u_{t}
as in Theorem
C.1
we derive

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int_{\Gamma}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\left(\frac{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}{e^{n\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})}-1}\right)+\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int_{\Gamma}\langle\log u_{t},\nu\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{t}^{n}

\displaystyle=\gamma(\mathcal{T})-\nu_{t}(\mathcal{T})+\langle\log u_{t},\nu_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma).

Subsequently, we can compute as follows:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{0}|\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\int_{\Gamma}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{0}}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{0}}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}}\right)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma),

and hence the initial data are well-prepared. Therefore, we can conclude for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}|\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\int_{\Gamma}\phi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n,\nu_{t}}}\right)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}^{n}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma)-\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma)=0,

thus establishing the entropic propagation of chaos result.
∎

Appendix A
Motivation from large deviations

In Section
3
, we introduced a new generalized gradient structure for the forward Kolmogorov equation and later showed convergence in the large-population limit to a structure that was lifted from the mean-field dynamics. Here we briefly discuss the relation between existing variational structures, and their connection to the asymptotic probabilities of the underlying process as treated in large deviation theory. All calculations are purely formal and are meant for illustratory purposes.

Throughout, for simplicity, let
\mathcal{T}
be a finite set. Recall the reacting particle system formulation described by (
1.2
), i.e. as particles
A_{t}^{1},\dots,A_{t}^{N_{t}}\in\mathcal{T}
at positions
X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N_{t}}\in\mathcal{T}
, and with

\displaystyle A^{i}_{t}

\displaystyle\to A^{i}_{t}+A^{N_{t}+1}_{t}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{with rate}

\displaystyle m\left(X_{t}^{i},X_{t}^{N_{1}+1}\right)\gamma\left(X_{t}^{N_{1}+1}\right),

\displaystyle A^{i}_{t}+A^{j}_{t}

\displaystyle\to A^{j}_{t}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{with rate}

\displaystyle n^{-1}c\left(X^{i}_{t},X^{j}_{t}\right).

Let
L_{t}^{n}
be the rescaled empirical measure

L_{t}^{n}(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{t}}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}(x),

and
W_{t}^{n,\pm}
the
integrated birth/death fluxes
:

W^{n,\pm}_{t}(x):=\frac{1}{n}\#\Big\{\mbox{Number of births($+$)/deaths($-$) at position $x$ in the time-window $[0,t)$}\Big\}.

Moreover, assume that the particles are initially distributed at time
t=0
as
\pi_{n}
. Then by the work of
[
PR19
]
, one can derive under suitable assumptions that the triple
(L_{t}^{n},W_{t}^{n,\pm})
is a well-defined Markov process and satisfies a
large-deviation principle
as
n\to\infty
with rate function
\mathcal{I}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{+})
in the sense that asymptotically (as
n\to\infty
)

\mathrm{Prob}\left(L_{t}^{n}\approx\nu_{t},\,W_{t}^{n,\pm}\approx\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{s}^{\pm}\,\mathrm{d}s,\,\forall t\in[0,T]\right)\asymp e^{-n\left(\mathcal{I}^{0}(\nu_{0})+\mathcal{I}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\right)}

where
\mathcal{I}^{0}(\nu):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu|\gamma)
and

\mathcal{I}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-})\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mathcal{L}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{+}|\kappa_{\nu}^{+})+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\lambda^{-}|\kappa_{\nu}^{-}).

Now, under the detailed balance assumption
m(x,y)=c(y,x)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{T}
, one can show that if
\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0})<\infty
the rate function
\mathcal{I}
is precisely the mean-field EDP-functional defined in (
2.4
):

\mathcal{I}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})=\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}).

This can be seen via symmetrization under
time-reversal
. Note that for any curve
(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\in\mathscr{CE}
the ‘reversed’ curve
(\nu_{T-t},\lambda^{-}_{T-t},\lambda^{+}_{T-t})
is still contained in
\mathscr{CE}
, and

\mathcal{I}^{{\dagger}}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-}):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}(\nu_{T-t},\lambda^{-}_{T-t},\lambda^{+}_{T-t})\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Then for suitable curves we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{I}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})+\mathcal{I}^{{\dagger}}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})+\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu_{t})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{I}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})-\mathcal{I}^{{\dagger}}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{T})-\mathcal{F}_{MF}(\nu_{0}),

which follows from the fact that if
\mathcal{L}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{-})
and
\mathcal{L}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t},\lambda_{t}^{+})
are finite

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})+\mathcal{L}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})+\mathcal{D}_{MF}(\nu),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t})-\mathcal{L}_{MF}(\nu_{t},\lambda^{-}_{t},\lambda^{+}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\,\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{t}|\gamma).

The splitting above is a direct consequence of fact that under the assumption of
c(x,x)=0
,
m(x,y)=c(y,x)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{T}
, the underlying jump process
L_{t}^{n}
is reversible, i.e.,
\bar{\kappa}_{n}
satisfies the detailed balance condition
\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)=\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\nu)
. Namely, consider the functional
\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
given by

\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\mathsf{P},j):=\int_{0}^{T}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},j)\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},j_{t}):=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(j_{t}|\mathsf{P}_{t}\bar{\kappa}_{n}),

where
j(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)\in\mathcal{M}^{+}(\Gamma\times\Gamma)
and
(\mathsf{P}_{t}\bar{\kappa}_{n})
is short-hand for the measure
\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)
. Let
j^{{\dagger}}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta):=j(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\nu)
, which again corresponds to a time-reversal procedure. We then have for suitable
(\mathsf{P},j)
the following decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},j_{t})+\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},j^{{\dagger}}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(j_{t}|\bar{\Theta}^{n}_{\mathsf{P}_{t}})+2H^{2}(\mathsf{P}_{t}\bar{\kappa}_{n},\bar{\kappa}_{n}\mathsf{P}_{t}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},j_{t})-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathsf{P}_{t},j^{{\dagger}}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\,\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{t}|\Pi_{n}),

where

\bar{\Theta}^{n}_{\mathsf{P}}:=\sqrt{(\mathsf{P}\bar{\kappa}_{n})(\bar{\kappa}_{n}\mathsf{P})},

and
(\bar{\kappa}_{n}\mathsf{P})
is short-hand for the measure
\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\nu)
. Now substituting

j_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta):=n\int_{x\in\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\mathsf{J}^{+}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)+n\int_{x\in\mathcal{T}}\delta_{\nu-\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\,\mathsf{J}^{-}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x),

we find that

(A.1)

\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}(\mathsf{P},j)=n\,\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(j_{t}|\mathsf{P}_{t}\bar{\kappa}_{n}),\qquad\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\mathsf{P},j)=n\,\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-}).

And as we have shown, in the large-population limit of
n\to\infty
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}
EDP-converges to a functional that is lifted from
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
, establishing the microscopic origin of the splitting for
\mathcal{I}_{MF}
.

This decomposition for reversible processes is well-known in the net-flux representation. Namely, one can show via a minimization approach that

\inf_{j}\left\{\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(j|\bar{\Theta}^{n}_{\mathsf{P}})\,:\,\int_{\eta\in\Gamma}\left(j(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}\eta)-j(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\nu)\right)=\mathrm{j}^{net}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\right\}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma^{2}}\Psi\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}j^{\mathrm{net}}}{\mathrm{d}\bar{\Theta}^{n}_{\mathsf{P}}}\right)\mathrm{d}\bar{\Theta}^{n}_{\mathsf{P}},

using a dualization argument and the elementary equality

(A.2)

\Psi(e^{z}-e^{-z})=\phi(e^{z})+\phi(e^{-z}),\qquad\mbox{ for all }z\in\mathbb{R}.

Thus
\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
is simply the EDP-functional for jump processes of
[
PRST22
]
. The works
[
MPR14
,
KJZ18
,
PS22
]
contain an extensive overview and discussion on how
\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
is the expected rate functional for a large-deviation principle for the empirical measures of independent jump processes, how the reversibility of the process ensures a possible splitting in both the interacting and non-interacting case, and how for complex-balanced systems this can even be done in the irreversible setting. Moreover, for an implicit decomposition using measure-dependent Dirichlet forms in the case of the homogeneous Boltzmann equation and the underlying process, see
[
BBBO21
]
.

On a final note, due to (
A.1
) and the origin of
\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
in large deviations for independent particles (or via variational representations as found in
[
DE97
]
), one would expect that if
F_{t}\in C_{b}(\Gamma)
for all
t\in[0,T]
, we would have for all
n>0
the following representation formula for the expectation:

\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-n\int_{0}^{T}F_{t}(L_{t}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}t}\right]=\inf_{(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})}\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}F_{t}(\nu_{t})\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{0}|\Pi_{n})+\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\right\}.

On the other hand, by the large deviation principle of
(L_{t}^{n},W_{t}^{n,\pm})
as
n\to\infty
, and Varadhan’s Lemma (see
[
DZ10
]
), it holds that

-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-n\int_{0}^{T}F_{t}(L_{t}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}t}\right]\\
=\inf_{(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})}\left\{\int_{0}^{T}F(\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma)+\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\right\}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\inf_{(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})}\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}F_{t}(\nu_{t})\mathsf{P}_{t}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}_{0}|\Pi_{n})+\mathcal{I}_{n}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{J}^{+},\mathsf{J}^{-})\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt=\inf_{(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})}\left\{\int_{0}^{T}F(\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\nu_{0}|\gamma)+\mathcal{I}_{MF}(\nu,\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\right\}.

Note that the lower bound of this equality follows from Theorem
5.1
and the superposition principle in Theorem
4.7
. Moreover,
we expect that the large-deviation principle implies evolutionary
\varGamma
-convergence of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
in a suitable topology—an implication studied in
[
Kra19
]
in a general setting.

It then begs the question if one can reverse this procedure, namely using evolutionary
\varGamma
-convergence to establish large-deviation principles similar to the non-evolutionary setting of
[
Mar12
]
. This approach was successfully applied in the case of certain diffusion processes
[
Fat16
]
and discussed for more general processes in
[
KJZ19
]
.

Appendix B
Superposition principle in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}

In this section, we present a superposition principle for continuity equations over
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
with an additional weighted integrability condition on the associated vector fields.

Following
[
AT14
, Section 7]
, we equip
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
with the product topology, and
\pi_{n}:=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
the canonical projections. The space
AC_{w}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
consists of curves
\eta
such that
p_{i}\circ\eta\in AC[0,T]
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that both
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
are Polish spaces. Moreover, let
|\cdot|_{\infty}
be the uniform norm on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Smooth
n
-cylindrical functions with compact support
f:\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}
are given in the form of

f(x)=\phi(\pi_{n}(x))=\phi(p_{1}(x),\dots,p_{n}(x)),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}},

with
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R})
, and define their gradient by

\nabla f(x):=\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial z_{1}}(\pi_{n}(x)),\dots,\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial z_{n}}(\pi_{n}(x)),0,0,\dots\right).

We set
\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
as the union over
n\in\mathbb{N}
of all smooth
n
-cylindrical functions with compact support.

In the following, we consider pairs
(\nu,\boldsymbol{c})
, where
(\nu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
is a weakly continuous family of probability measures and
\boldsymbol{c}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a Borel vector field satisfying

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{r},\nabla f)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\qquad\mbox{for all $f\in\mathrm{Cyl}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})$,}

and all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.

We then have the following result.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
(\nu,\boldsymbol{c})
be as above. Furthermore, suppose that for some
M>0

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}M\Psi\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{t}|_{\infty}}{M(1+|p_{1}|)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Then there exists a Borel probability measure
\lambda
over
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
satisfying
(e_{t})_{\#}\lambda=\nu_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and is concentrated on the family of curves
\gamma\in AC([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
that satisfy

\dot{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{c}_{t}(\gamma),\qquad\text{for almost every $t\in[0,T]$.}

The proof of Theorem
B.1
combines a slight adaptation of the proof for the superposition principle in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
found in
[
AT14
, Theorem 7.1]
, developed for use in metric measure spaces, with a finite-dimensional result for vector fields over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
found in
[
AC08
, Theorem 4.4]
. Due to the strong similarities with the proof found in
[
AT14
]
, we merely give a brief sketch.

Proof.

By tightness of
\nu_{0}
, we can choose a sequence of coercive functionals
\Phi_{i}
such that

\int\Phi_{i}(p_{i}(x))\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}\leq 2^{-i},\qquad\mbox{for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$},

and consider the functional
\mathcal{A}(\eta):C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})\to[0,+\infty]
given by

\mathcal{A}(\eta):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\Phi_{i}(p_{i}\circ\eta(0))+\int_{0}^{T}M\Psi\left(\frac{|\dot{\eta}(t)|_{\infty}}{M(1+|p_{1}\circ\eta(t)|)}\right)\mathrm{d}t\right)\quad&&\mbox{if $\eta\in AC_{w}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})$,}\\
&+\infty\quad&&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

It is clear that
\mathcal{A}
is coercive in
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
, and its sublevel sets contain curves that are absolutely continuous with respect to
|\cdot|_{\infty}
. This follows from the fact that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{|p_{1}\circ\eta|}
is bounded on the sublevel sets of the functional

\left(\Phi_{1}(p_{1}\circ\eta(0))+\int_{0}^{T}M\Psi\left(\frac{|(p_{1}\circ\eta)^{\prime}(t)|}{M(1+|p_{1}\circ\eta(t)|)}\right)\mathrm{d}t\right).

Now, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the marginals
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni\nu^{n}_{t}:=(\pi_{n})_{\#}\nu_{t}
and corresponding vector fields
\boldsymbol{c}_{t}^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}_{t}^{n}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}\,(\pi_{n})_{\#}\left((p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}_{t})\,\nu_{t}\right)}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}^{n}}.

Note that
(\nu^{n},\boldsymbol{c}_{t}^{n})
satisfies the continuity equation in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By Jensen’s inequality, and the fact that
|z_{1}|\leq|z|\leq n|z|_{\infty}
, for
z=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have that

\displaystyle T\Psi\left(\frac{1}{nMT}\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{t}|}{(1+|x_{1}|)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq T\Psi\left(\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{t}|_{\infty}}{MT(1+|x_{1}|)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int\Psi\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{t}|_{\infty}}{M(1+|x_{1}|)}\right)\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

and in particular

\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{|\boldsymbol{c}_{t}|}{(1+|x_{1}|)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Hence, we can apply the finite-dimensional version of
[
AC08
, Theorem 4.4]
. Embedding this into
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we obtain the probability measure
\lambda_{n}
over
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
, concentrated on absolutely continuous curves satisfying
\dot{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{c}_{t}^{n}(\gamma)
, and such that
(e_{t})_{\#}\lambda_{t}=\nu_{t}^{n}
. We immediately see that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int\mathcal{A}(\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{n}(\gamma)<\infty,

which yields the tightness of
\lambda^{n}
.

Consider any converging sequence
\lambda^{n}
(up to renumbering) and its limit
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}))
. Since the sequence
(\nu_{t}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
clearly converges to
\nu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\#}\lambda
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
for every
t\in[0,T]
, it remains to show that
\lambda
is concentrated on solutions of
\dot{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{c}_{t}(\gamma)
. In fact we will show that

\int\frac{\left|p_{i}\circ\gamma(t)-p_{i}\circ\gamma(s)-\int_{s}^{t}p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}_{r}(\gamma(r))\,\mathrm{d}r\right|}{1+\|p_{1}\circ\gamma\|_{\infty}}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}\gamma)=0\qquad\text{for each $i\in\mathbb{N}$ and any $0\leq s\leq t\leq T$.}

Note that it suffices to show that for any vector field
d:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}
with
d_{t}
being
k
-cylindrical for every
t\in[0,T]
, we have that

(B.1)

\int\frac{\left|p_{i}\circ\gamma(t)-p_{i}\circ\gamma(s)-\int_{s}^{t}d_{r}(\gamma(r))\,\mathrm{d}r\right|}{1+\|p_{1}\circ\gamma\|_{\infty}}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\leq\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{|p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}_{r}-d_{r}|}{1+|p_{1}|}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r,

since then we can use density of time-dependent cylindrical functions in
L^{1}((1+|p_{1}|)^{-1}\nu_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s)
and the fact that for all
s
it holds that
|p_{1}\circ\gamma(s)|\leq\|p_{1}\circ\gamma\|_{\infty}
.

To prove (
B.1
), recall that
\lambda^{n}
is concentrated on absolutely continuous solutions of
\dot{\gamma}_{s}=\boldsymbol{c}^{n}_{s}(\gamma_{s})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int\frac{\left|p_{i}\circ\gamma(t)-p_{i}\circ\gamma(0)-\int_{0}^{t}d_{s}(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\right|}{1+\|p_{1}\circ\gamma\|_{\infty}}\lambda^{n}(\mathrm{d}\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\left|\int_{0}^{t}(p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}^{n}_{s}(\gamma(s))-d_{s}(\gamma(s)))\,\mathrm{d}s\right|}{1+\|p_{1}\circ\gamma\|_{\infty}}\lambda^{n}(\mathrm{d}\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\int\frac{\int_{0}^{t}|p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}^{n}_{s}-d_{s}|(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s}{1+|p_{1}(\gamma(s))|}\lambda^{n}(\mathrm{d}\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{|p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}^{n}_{s}-d_{s}|}{1+|p_{1}|}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{n}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s

Note that the integrand on the left-hand side is continuous in
\gamma
. Therefore, since for
n\geq k

(p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}_{s}^{n}-d_{s})\nu_{s}^{n}=(\pi_{n})_{\#}((p_{i}\circ\boldsymbol{c}_{s}-d_{s})\nu_{s}),

the result then follows after taking the limit
n\to\infty
.
∎

Remark B.2
.

If one is only interested in curves in
AC_{w}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
, the theorem also holds whenever

\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{|p_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}_{t})|}{1+|p_{1}|}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\qquad\mbox{for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$}.

The finite dimensional analog of this statement, set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the prefactor
(1+|x|)^{-1}
, is presented in
[
AC08
, Theorem 4.4]
. Moreover, for
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, in
[
AT14
, Theorem 7.1]
the condition reads as

\int_{0}^{T}\int|p_{i}(\boldsymbol{c}_{t})|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\qquad\mbox{for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$}.

Appendix C
Non-continuous competition kernel

In the proof of Theorem
5.1
we require the vague convergence of
\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{\pm}
and
\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{\pm}
under the assumption of narrow convergence of
\mathsf{P}^{n}
and equiboundedness of the free energy functionals
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, where

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{y\in\mathcal{T}}c(x,y)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}}^{-}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{y\in\mathcal{T}}c(x,y)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

If the competition kernel
c
is continuous, the desired statement would follow directly from the narrow convergence of
\mathsf{P}^{n}
. The case of merely bounded measurable
c
is however less trivial. Note that the strategy we employed in the proof of Theorem
3.20
is not possible, since although for every fixed
n
the sub-levels of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
are sequentially compact with respect to setwise convergence, this is not the case for equibounded sets of
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
.

Fortunately, due to the connection between
\varGamma
-convergence of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and large deviations as discussed in Section
A
, we can modify results from the authors’ earlier work on large deviations for interacting systems induced by singular or irregular functionals
[
HHMT20
]
. In particular, we obtain the following convergence statement.

Theorem C.1
.

Let
\{\mathsf{P}^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
be a sequence narrowly converging to
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
with

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n})<\infty.

Then for any
\omega\in C_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
and
g\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T}^{2})

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

Remark C.2
.

The result can be easily generalized to bounded measurable functions
g\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T}^{k})
for finite
k\in\mathbb{N}
, but we restrict ourselves to the case
k=2
.

Corollary C.3
.

Let
\{\mathsf{P}^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
be a sequence narrowly converging to
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma)
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n})<\infty.

Then vaguely

(C.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}=\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}},\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{T}^{n,\pm}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{\pm}=\vartheta^{\pm}_{\mathsf{P}}.

Proof.

The first statement of (
C.1
) follows directly from Theorem
C.1
by substituting
g:=c
. Moreover, by the uniform continuity and compact support of any
\omega\in C_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}\times\Gamma}\omega\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{T}^{n,+}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{+}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}\times\Gamma}\omega(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\,\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}\nu,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g(x,y)\omega(\nu+\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\delta_{x},x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

and a similar approach works for
\mathsf{T}^{n,-}_{\#}\vartheta_{\mathsf{P}^{n}}^{-}
.
∎

For the proof of Theorem (
C.1
) we will need some a priori bounds. Namely, recall from Section
5.1
the generating functionals and their limit

G_{n}(f):=\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{\Gamma}e^{n\langle f,\nu\rangle}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu),\quad G(f):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}(e^{f}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma.

For the “interacting” case, namely functionals of the form

\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{\Gamma}e^{n\langle g,\nu^{\otimes 2}\rangle}\,\Pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

there is however a problem with the unboundedness of the mass of
\nu
. Nevertheless, upon controlling the mass we can provide the following technical estimate.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
F(\nu):=h(\nu(\mathcal{T}))\langle g,\nu^{\otimes 2}\rangle
with
\mathrm{supp}(h)\in[0,K]
and
g\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T}^{2})
. Then

(C.2)

\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\log\int_{\Gamma}e^{n|F|}\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{4K\|h\|_{\infty}|g|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{n}\left(K\|g\|_{\infty}\|h\|_{\infty}-\log(e^{n\gamma(X)}-1)\right),

and in particular

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{\Gamma}e^{n|F|}\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}\leq\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{2K\|h\|_{\infty}|g|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)^{1/2}.

Proof.

Suppose that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(\mathsf{P}^{n})=:C<\infty,

and let us consider the following interaction energy functional:

E_{g}^{N}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}):=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j\neq i}|g|(x_{i},x_{j}).

From a Hoeffding’s decomposition argument, see
[
HHMT20
, Lemma 3.8]
, we have for every
N\geq 2
,
\alpha\geq 0
the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\log\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}e^{\alpha NE_{g}^{N}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}\leq\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}}e^{\frac{2\alpha N}{N-1}|g|(x,y)}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right).

Moreover, since
N/(N-1)\leq 2
for
N\geq 2
, and

\sum_{i,j}|g|(x_{i},x_{j})=\sum_{i,j\neq i}|g|(x_{i},x_{j})+\sum_{i}|g|(x_{i},x_{i})\leq\sum_{i,j\neq i}|g|(x_{i},x_{j})+N\|g\|_{\infty},

we find that

\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}e^{\frac{\alpha}{N}\sum_{i,j}|g|(x_{i},x_{j})}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}}e^{4\alpha|g|(x,y)}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)+\frac{\alpha\|g\|_{\infty}}{N}.

Recall that
L_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}):=\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n}\sum\delta_{x_{i}}
. Since the mass
L_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})(\mathcal{T})=N/n
is bounded by
K
on the support of
F
we have for
N\geq 2
:

|F|(L_{n})\leq|h|(L_{n}(\mathcal{T}))\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{1}{n^2}\sum_{i,j}|g|(x_{i},x_{j})\leq\frac{K\|h\|_{\infty}}{nN}\sum_{i,j}|g|(x_{i},x_{j}),

while for
N=1
we have the trivial estimate
|F|(L_{n})\leq\genfrac{}{}{}{2}{K}{n}\|h\|_{\infty}\|g\|_{\infty}
, and hence for all
N\geq 1
,

\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}e^{n|F|(L_{N})}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}\leq e^{K\|g\|_{\infty}\|h\|_{\infty}}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{4K\|h\|_{\infty}|g|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)^{N/2}.

Using the representation for
\Pi_{n}
we can therefore estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}e^{n|F|}\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(n\gamma(\mathcal{T}))^{N}}{N!}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}e^{n|F|}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes N}/\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{N}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{(n\gamma(\mathcal{T}))^{N}}{N!}e^{K\|g\|_{\infty}\|h\|_{\infty}}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{4K\|h\|_{\infty}|g|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)^{N/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{K\|g\|_{\infty}\|h\|_{\infty}}}{e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1}\exp\left\{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})\left(\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{4K\|h\|_{\infty}|g|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)^{1/2}\right\},

which proves (
C.2
). The final desired statement follows directly after taking limits.
∎

With the above estimate in hand, we can now prove our convergence statement by approximating
g
with a sequence of continuous
g_{\varepsilon}
such that

(C.3)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{\beta|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}=0,\qquad\mbox{ for all }\beta>0.

The existence of such a sequence follows similarly as for density statements in
L^{p}(\gamma)
, see for example
[
HHMT20
]
[Theorem C.5].

Proof of Theorem
C.1
.

Consider a
g\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{T}^{2})
and let
\{g_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}\subset C_{b}(\mathcal{T}^{2})
be a sequence approximating
g
in the sense of (
C.3
). Note that by the narrow convergence of
\mathsf{P}^{n}
we have for any
\omega\in C_{c}(\Gamma\times\mathcal{T})
and any
\varepsilon>0
that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}\times\Gamma}g_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\omega(\nu,x)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathsf{P}(\mathrm{d}\nu).

Note that by the compact support of
\omega
, it suffices to show that for every
K>0
:

(C.4a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\nu(\mathcal{T})\leq K}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(x,y)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=0,

(C.4b)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\nu(\mathcal{T})\leq K}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(x,y)\gamma(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)=0.

Let us consider (
C.4a
), and set

F_{\varepsilon,K}(\nu):=1_{\nu(\mathcal{T})\leq K}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(x,y)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y).

By duality of the entropy and Lemma
C.4
, we have for every
n\geq 1,\varepsilon>0,K>0
, and
\beta>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}F_{\varepsilon,K}(\nu)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\beta n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}|\Pi_{n})+\frac{1}{\beta n}\log\int_{\Gamma}e^{n\beta F_{\varepsilon,K}}\,\mathrm{d}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\beta n}\mathcal{E}\mathrm{nt}(\mathsf{P}^{n}|\Pi_{n})+\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{4\beta K|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\beta n}\left(K\beta\|g-g_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}-\log(e^{n\gamma(\mathcal{T})}-1)\right).

Taking subsequently the limits
n\to\infty
and
\varepsilon\to 0
, we deduce

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma}F_{\varepsilon,K}(\nu)\,\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)\leq\frac{C}{\beta}.

But, since
\beta>0
was arbitrary, we conclude that the right-hand side reduces to zero.

Similarly, for (
C.4b
), let

f_{\varepsilon}(x):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(y,x)\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}y),\qquad F_{\varepsilon}(\nu):=\int_{\mathcal{T}}f_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x).

Then by duality and Lemma
5.6
we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma}F_{\varepsilon,K}(\nu)\mathsf{P}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\beta}+\int_{\mathcal{T}}(e^{\beta f_{\varepsilon}(x)}-1)\mathrm{d}\gamma\leq\frac{C}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\gamma(\mathcal{T})}\int_{\mathcal{T}^{2}}e^{\beta\gamma(\mathcal{T})|g-g_{\varepsilon}|(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\otimes 2},

where the last inequality follows by applying Jensen’s inequality inside the exponential. Again taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
and thereafter
\beta\to\infty
we conclude the proof.
∎
